The route guard is `authenticateToken` only (l.619), with no requireRole. Tenant scoping itself is correct — organization_id is the real key and the comment at l.638-642 documents fixing a previously unscoped query — so this is exposure within a tenant, not across tenants.

**Failure scenario.** A 'member'-role user calls GET /api/billing/activity and receives the raw checkout.session.completed event for their organization, including customer_details (billing name, full postal address, email) and the collected tax IDs — commercial data an org would normally restrict to admins/finance. Note that stripe_events is also on the RLS allowlist (server/db/rlsAllowlist.ts:63) because organization_id is nullable, so there is no database-layer backstop if the application filter regresses.

**Fix.** Project only the fields the activity feed renders (event_type, timestamp, formatted message) instead of returning `payload`, and gate the endpoint on requireRole('admin','owner') like the other billing mutations.

#### `P3` BILL-23 — The Business Center 'tier pricing' rate card has no effect on what Stripe actually charges

`server/routes/admin/business/rate-cards.ts:144` · blocks **G2** · ~days · claims-vs-code

**Evidence.** PATCH /tier-pricing/:tier upserts into `tier_pricing` (l.162-172) and is consumed only by loadTierPrices → buildCostAccounting's modeled revenue (server/routes/admin/business/cost-model.ts:52-66, 132-133). The amounts Stripe charges come from hardcoded constants in a different module that never reads tier_pricing: DTC_PRICING (server/services/billing.ts:69-122) and PRICING (l.158-287).

**Failure scenario.** The owner decides to raise Standard from $499 to $599, opens the Business Center rate card and PATCHes tier-pricing/standard with monthlyPriceCents 59900 and a reason. The change is audited and the P&L immediately reports higher MRR — but every subsequent Stripe Checkout still creates a $499 price from DTC_PRICING. The company now under-charges relative to its own reported revenue and the discrepancy is invisible because the P&L reports the modeled (new) number for any org without Stripe invoices.

**Fix.** Make billing.ts read the tier price card at checkout time (with the constants as seed defaults), or relabel the surface unambiguously as 'recognized revenue model — does not change customer pricing' in both the API response and the admin UI.

#### `P3` BILL-24 — Referral program advertised on the billing page ('$10 in usage credits') has no implementation and a dead button

`client/src/concept2cure/v2/surfaces/UsageBilling.tsx:474` · blocks **G1** · ~hours · honest-states

**Evidence.** 'Give AnA, get more AnA — Send a colleague a free week. If they subscribe, you both get $10 in usage credits. Terms apply.' with `<button className="sp-ask">{I.link || I.copy} Copy link</button>` and no onClick (l.474-488). There is no referral code, no referral table, no route and no credit-ledger entry type for it anywhere in server/ (CREDIT_ENTRY_TYPES is grant|purchase|auto_reload|debit|adjustment, server/services/credit-ledger.ts:38), and no 'Terms apply' document exists.

**Failure scenario.** A pilot user reads the offer, clicks 'Copy link', nothing is copied and no link exists. If they nonetheless refer a colleague who subscribes, neither party receives $10 because no code path can create such a ledger entry. This is a commercial inducement rendered in-product with no backing implementation and no terms.

**Fix.** Remove the card until the referral program exists, or implement it (referral code issuance, attribution on signup, and a credit-ledger grant on the referee's first payment) with linked terms.

#### `P3` BILL-25 — Billing and entitlement state changes are not written to the Part 11 audit chain

`server/services/billing.ts:734` · blocks **G3** · ~days · part11-audit

**Evidence.** handleSubscriptionUpdated (l.734-758), handleSubscriptionDeleted (l.770-778), handlePaymentSucceeded (l.791-798), handlePaymentFailed (l.809-815) and provisionModulesForTier (l.867-873) all mutate organizations.tier / payment_status / max_users / max_projects and module_subscriptions with bare pool.query and no audit write. The unit demonstrably knows how to do this correctly elsewhere — setSeatsPurchased writes a previousValue/newValue logAuditEvent (server/services/seat-licensing.ts:159-175), setWeeklyLimit and setAutoReload do the same, and rate-card edits call auditService.logAction (rate-cards.ts:96-109, 174-188). Only module ACCESS DENIALS are audited (license-manager.ts:363-372) — grants are not.

**Failure scenario.** A GxP customer's qualification auditor asks who changed org 42 from professional to free on 2026-03-11 and which modules were revoked. There is no audit record: the only trace is the raw stripe_events payload (if it was stored at all) and a mutated organizations row with an updated_at timestamp and no actor, no before-value and no reason. Entitlement changes that gate access to validated functionality are therefore not reconstructable, failing Part 11 §11.10(e).

**Fix.** Route every billing/entitlement state change through logAuditEvent with previousValue/newValue/actor (system) and the triggering Stripe event id, matching the pattern already used by seat-licensing and weekly-usage-limits.

### Infrastructure, CI/CD & Operability

This unit is two very different codebases wearing one name. The CI *governance* layer is genuinely strong and unusual: ~32 of 39 `scripts/ci` checkers run and block on every PR, the DB-backed test jobs run against real pgvector Postgres with migrations applied, and the `blank-db-provisioning` job is a best-in-class proof that the from-scratch install and deploy-migration paths actually work. The production *deployment* layer, by contrast, has never worked and cannot work as committed: I proved empirically that the terraform-defined ECS task definition omits four environment variables the application fail-closes on at import time (MFA_ENCRYPTION_KEY, RLS_ENFORCE, AUDIT_HMAC_KEY, REFRESH_TOKEN_SECRET), and `deploy-aws.yml` builds `-f worker/Dockerfile` against a `worker/` directory that does not exist in the repository. Three independent health-check definitions (ALB target group, ECS container check, Helm probes) all point at `/api/health`, which the repo's own Dockerfile documents as a static 200 that passes against a down or unmigrated database, so a broken task stays in ALB rotation indefinitely. Disaster recovery is absent in the strict sense — zero `aws_cloudwatch_metric_alarm`, zero `aws_sns_topic`, zero `aws_backup`, zero cross-region or cross-account copy, a single NAT gateway, and no rehearsed restore — while `docs/operations/audit-log-retention-policy.md` and `docs/SOP_KEY_MANAGEMENT.md` make specific customer-facing commitments (Glacier cross-account archive, RPO 0 / RTO 1h) that no code implements. The dominant risk for a buyer is that the seller's CI green is real for code quality and entirely notional for shippability: the only production deploy pipeline is broken in at least two independent places, the lint gate literally cannot fail (6,273 warnings, 0 errors, exit 0, proven by running it), the nightly governance job fails deterministically (proven, exit 1), and the alerting estate is six rules for one subsystem whose runbook links all 404.

**Genuine strengths in this unit**

- The `blank-db-provisioning` CI job (.github/workflows/ci.yml:554-681) is the single best thing in this unit and is better than most commercial products: it asserts the fixture database is genuinely empty, proves `scripts/db/deploy-migrate.mjs` REFUSES an unprovisioned database with a distinct exit code 3 and creates zero tables while refusing, provisions from scratch, proves the migration then succeeds, proves it is idempotent on re-run, and then re-verifies the readiness contract independently from psql (11 authoring tables, 4 tenant-parentage FKs, 11 RLS policies, the CRDT state table, >=8 rbm_* and >=6 cre_* tables) so a regression in the script's own verification cannot mask a schema regression.
- The digest-pinned register-then-deploy ECS pattern (.github/workflows/deploy-aws.yml:174-224, 461-524) captures the image content digest at push time and registers a task-def revision pinned to `<repo>@sha256:<digest>`, then updates the service by that revision ARN — the only shape that makes rollback deterministic. It is backed by a real CI guard, `scripts/check_no_force_new_deployment.sh`, enforced as a blocking step at ci.yml:114-119, that refuses any PR reintroducing `--force-new-deployment`.
- The production migration job (.github/workflows/deploy-aws.yml:245-438) runs `deploy-migrate.mjs` as a one-off Fargate task derived from the API task definition — same execution role, same DATABASE_URL secret, same log config — with sidecars, portMappings, healthCheck, dependsOn/links/volumesFrom deliberately stripped and the rationale documented; it reads networkConfiguration off the live service rather than duplicating subnet IDs, waits for the task, tails the CloudWatch log stream on both success and failure, and distinguishes exit 3 (never provisioned) from generic failure with an actionable remediation message.
- The fail-closed production boot posture in server/config/environment.ts (lines 203-292) is genuinely strong and unusual: a dedicated REFRESH_TOKEN_SECRET is required and must differ from JWT_SECRET; MFA_ENCRYPTION_KEY is mandatory and must not be JWT-derived; RLS_ENFORCE must be an explicit operator decision (unset refuses to boot rather than silently defaulting to off); and AUDIT_HMAC_KEY must be provisioned or the operator must record accepted risk via AUDIT_SEAL_ACCEPT_UNSEALED. Each assertion fires at module import so a misconfigured deploy cannot half-start. (That the deployment manifests do not satisfy them is BOOT-01, but the contract itself is right.)
- `/readyz` (server/startup/inline-endpoints.ts:63-118) is a real readiness endpoint: it probes the pool, reads a boot-time schema verdict, and conditionally checks Redis and the Bull worker tier only when REDIS_URL is configured — with an honest 'skipped' rather than a fake 'ok'. server/startup/readiness-state.ts defaults to 'unknown' (treated as ready) so only a POSITIVE 'missing' verdict turns readiness red, which is the correct fail-direction reasoning.
- The structured logger (server/utils/logger.ts) redacts above the pino layer with a depth-6 recursive walker so arbitrarily nested sensitive fields are scrubbed, with pino's own `redact.paths` retained as a backstop for callers that bypass the wrapper. The HTTP logger (server/src/mw/observability.ts:60-79) emits requestId, method, path, statusCode and durationMs at the correct severity per status class. The Sentry integration (server/utils/sentry.ts) sets sendDefaultPii:false, strips auth/cookie/x-org-id headers and user email/username/IP, and — correctly — returns null to DROP the event if the scrubber itself throws.
- The CI governance gate set is dense and real: 32 of 39 scripts/ci checkers block on every PR, covering classes most repos never gate (unbacked tables, unreferenced modules, tenant-column types, RLS allowlist TS/SQL parity, requestDb adoption, jwt.verify algorithm pinning, SAML fail-closed, no dev-auth in prod paths, no mock data in prod routes, AI-gateway bypass, regulated-delete audit coverage, DOCX/PDF/embedding runtime canonicality). The typecheck baseline has been ratcheted to 0, so any type error now fails CI.
- The DB-backed test jobs run against a real `pgvector/pgvector:pg15` service with the GCC migrations applied (ci.yml:236-312, 452-537), explicitly so that RLS/tenant-isolation/audit/migration suites EXECUTE rather than self-skipping on a missing DATABASE_URL — the comment at lines 240-246 names the false-assurance failure mode it fixes. The RLS rollout smoke additionally applies migrations 0019/0020/0021 against a separate fresh database to test the empty-schema path.
- Container and registry hygiene: Dockerfile.optimized is multi-stage, creates and runs as a non-root `appuser`, cleans apt lists, and deliberately ships both migration lineages plus scripts/db while deliberately excluding install-fresh.mjs (with the reasoning written down at lines 50-61). ECR repositories are `image_tag_mutability = "IMMUTABLE"` with `scan_on_push = true` and a lifecycle policy (terraform/modules/ecr/main.tf:3-49). `:latest` was removed from the deploy path and terraform's `image_tag` variable has a validation rule rejecting it.
- Network and TLS posture in terraform is mostly right where it counts: ECS tasks run in private subnets with `assign_public_ip = false` and an ingress rule scoped to the ALB security group only; the ALB enforces `ELBSecurityPolicy-TLS13-1-2-2021-06` with a 301 HTTP->HTTPS redirect; both ECS services enable `deployment_circuit_breaker { enable = true, rollback = true }`; and the CloudFront module uses Origin Access Control with a bucket policy conditioned on the distribution ARN plus a full public-access block (terraform/modules/cloudfront/main.tf:26-56).
- RDS is configured with real care: `manage_master_user_password = true` keeps the DB password out of terraform state entirely, plus storage_encrypted, multi_az, deletion_protection defaulting true, skip_final_snapshot=false with a named final snapshot, 35-day backup retention, Performance Insights, 60s enhanced monitoring with its own IAM role, and postgresql/upgrade log exports to CloudWatch.
- Terraform remote state is bootstrapped correctly and separately (terraform/bootstrap/main.tf): S3 with versioning, SSE-KMS, full public-access block and `prevent_destroy`, plus a DynamoDB lock table — the state layer itself is production-grade even where the stacks it holds are not.
- Dependency scanning is honest where it blocks: the Trivy filesystem scan is genuinely blocking on CRITICAL/HIGH in both ci.yml and deploy-aws.yml, `scripts/ci/audit-with-allowlist.mjs` filters only registry-lag false positives, and every .trivyignore suppression carries a written justification, a reachability argument and a TODO (e.g. the react-router RSC-CSRF entry explains that the app routes with wouter and the RSC path is unreachable). A CycloneDX SBOM of the production tree is published as a 90-day artifact.
- CodeQL runs on both javascript-typescript and python, on push, PR and weekly schedule, with `fail-fast: false`, a 45-minute timeout and full-depth checkout (.github/workflows/codeql.yml).
- Workflow-level hygiene is above average: every workflow except danger.yml declares a top-level `permissions:` block (danger.yml declares job-level instead), deploy-production uses `concurrency: { cancel-in-progress: false }` so a production deploy is never cancelled mid-flight, AWS access is OIDC role assumption rather than long-lived keys, and no workflow uses the dangerous `pull_request_target` trigger.

#### `P0` BOOT-01 — Terraform ECS task definition omits four env vars the app fail-closes on — the production container cannot boot

`terraform/modules/ecs-fargate/main.tf:138` · blocks **G1** · ~days · deployment-correctness

**Evidence.** Task def supplies only two env vars and three secrets:
```
environment = [
  { name = "NODE_ENV", value = "production" },
  { name = "PORT", value = tostring(var.api_container_port) },
]
secrets = [for s in var.api_secrets : ...]
```
and terraform/environments/production/main.tf:133-137 passes exactly `DATABASE_URL`, `JWT_SECRET`, `OPENAI_API_KEY`.

server/config/environment.ts fires four production assertions at MODULE IMPORT: `assertMfaKeyPosture()` (line 274), `assertRlsEnforcementForProduction()` (line 284), `assertAuditSealPostureForProduction()` (line 292), and `getRefreshTokenSecret()` via `config.jwt.refreshSecret` (line 314).

I ran this exact env set through the real module and walked the chain:
  NODE_ENV=production PORT=5000 DATABASE_URL=... JWT_SECRET=<48 chars> OPENAI_API_KEY=...
  -> '[FATAL] Missing or weak MFA_ENCRYPTION_KEY...'
  + MFA_ENCRYPTION_KEY -> '[rls-enforcement] REFUSING TO BOOT: RLS_ENFORCE is not set in production...'
  + RLS_ENFORCE=on -> '[audit-seal-posture] REFUSING TO BOOT: AUDIT_HMAC_KEY is not configured...'
  + AUDIT_HMAC_KEY -> '[FATAL] Missing required REFRESH_TOKEN_SECRET...'
  + REFRESH_TOKEN_SECRET -> config loaded OK

**Failure scenario.** An operator runs `terraform apply` in terraform/environments/production and then triggers deploy-aws.yml. The ECS API task starts, `server/index.ts` imports `server/config/environment.ts`, `assertMfaKeyPosture()` throws before the HTTP listener is created, the process exits non-zero. ECS restarts the task; it exits again. The ALB target group never registers a healthy target, `aws ecs wait services-stable` in deploy-api times out, and production serves 503 from the ALB with no application logs beyond the FATAL line. The same four vars are absent from docker-compose.yml:68-84 (the documented 'canonical self-host / install SKU'), so `docker compose up` with NODE_ENV=production fails identically; charts/concept2cure-app/values.yaml:97-104 supplies REFRESH_TOKEN_SECRET but not the other three.

**Fix.** Add MFA_ENCRYPTION_KEY, AUDIT_HMAC_KEY and REFRESH_TOKEN_SECRET to terraform/modules/secrets + the api_secrets/worker_secrets lists, and RLS_ENFORCE to the container `environment` block. Mirror into charts/concept2cure-app/values.yaml secret.data and docker-compose.yml. Then add a CI job that boots the built image with only the manifest-declared env and asserts a 200 from /readyz — the class of defect is 'no one has ever started this image from these manifests'.

#### `P0` DEPLOY-01 — Production deploy builds worker/Dockerfile, which does not exist in the repository

`.github/workflows/deploy-aws.yml:213` · blocks **G1** · ~days · deployment-correctness

**Evidence.** ```
- name: Build & push Worker image
  id: worker-push
  if: ${{ github.event.inputs.deploy_worker != 'false' }}
  run: |
    docker build -f worker/Dockerfile \
      -t "$ECR_REGISTRY/$ECR_WORKER_REPO:$IMAGE_TAG" .
```
`ls worker/` -> 'No such file or directory'. `find . -maxdepth 3 -iname 'Dockerfile*' -not -path './node_modules/*'` returns only ./Dockerfile.optimized and ./services/Dockerfile. Terraform nevertheless provisions the worker ECR repo (terraform/environments/production/main.tf:50), the worker ECS task definition and service (terraform/modules/ecs-fargate/main.tf:171-205, 241-260), and /readyz checks a Bull worker tier (server/startup/inline-endpoints.ts:92-102).

**Failure scenario.** A release engineer pushes tag `v1.1.0`. `github.event.inputs.deploy_worker` is empty on a tag push, so `'' != 'false'` evaluates true and the worker build step runs. `docker build -f worker/Dockerfile .` exits with 'failed to read dockerfile: open worker/Dockerfile: no such file or directory'. The build-push job fails, so `migrate`, `deploy-api` and `deploy-worker` (all `needs: build-push`) never run. No production release is possible through the documented tag path, and the API image that was already pushed to ECR is orphaned.

**Fix.** Either add the worker Dockerfile (and its entrypoint) to the repo, or delete the worker build step, the worker ECR repo, the worker task definition and the worker ECS service from terraform, and change /readyz to stop reporting a worker tier that has no image.

#### `P0` HEALTH-01 — ALB, ECS and Helm health checks all probe /api/health, a static 200 that passes against a down or unmigrated database

`terraform/modules/alb/main.tf:64` · blocks **G1** · ~hours · availability

**Evidence.** ALB target group: `health_check { enabled = true, path = "/api/health", matcher = "200" }` (terraform/modules/alb/main.tf:62-71).
ECS container check: `command = ["CMD-SHELL", "wget -qO- http://localhost:${var.api_container_port}/api/health || exit 1"]` (terraform/modules/ecs-fargate/main.tf:157-163).
Helm: `probes.path: /api/health` used for BOTH livenessProbe and readinessProbe (charts/concept2cure-app/values.yaml:70-75, templates/deployment.yaml:62-77).

The handler is unconditional:
```
app.get('/api/health', (_req, res) => {
  res.json({ status: 'healthy', timestamp: new Date().toISOString() });
});
```
(server/startup/inline-endpoints.ts:119-121)

The repo documents this itself in Dockerfile.optimized:76-78: 'Probe /readyz (not /api/health): /api/health is a static liveness ping that returns 200 even against a missing-schema DB, whereas /readyz fails closed on a down database or a positively-verified missing schema.' The Dockerfile HEALTHCHECK correctly uses /readyz — but ECS Fargate ignores image HEALTHCHECK instructions and honours only the task-definition `healthCheck`, so the correct probe is inert on the actual production platform. Secondary defect: the task-def command shells `wget`, which Dockerfile.optimized:26-35 never installs (it installs curl).

**Failure scenario.** RDS fails over, or a deploy ships code whose tables the migration task did not create. Every API task keeps answering `/api/health` with 200. The ALB keeps all tasks in rotation, the ECS `deployment_circuit_breaker` (terraform/modules/ecs-fargate/main.tf:228-231) never trips because the tasks report healthy, and users receive 500s from every data-backed route for the full duration of the outage. On Kubernetes the readinessProbe likewise never removes the pod from the Service. The one signal that would have caught it — /readyz, which returns 503 when `getSchemaReadiness() === 'missing'` or `pool.query('select 1')` throws — is wired to nothing that routes traffic.

**Fix.** Change the ALB target-group `health_check.path`, the ECS container `healthCheck` command and the Helm `readinessProbe.path` to `/readyz`. Keep `/api/health` (or `/healthz`) for the Helm livenessProbe only. Replace `wget` with `curl` (installed) or the Node one-liner already used in the Dockerfile HEALTHCHECK.

#### `P0` TF-DRIFT-01 — aws_ecs_service has no ignore_changes on task_definition — any terraform apply silently rolls production back to an older image

`terraform/modules/ecs-fargate/main.tf:209` · blocks **G1** · ~hours · deployment-correctness

**Evidence.** ```
resource "aws_ecs_service" "api" {
  name            = "${var.cluster_name}-api"
  cluster         = aws_ecs_cluster.this.id
  task_definition = aws_ecs_task_definition.api.arn
  ...
}
```
No `lifecycle { ignore_changes = [task_definition, desired_count] }` block exists anywhere in the module (lines 209-260). Meanwhile deploy-aws.yml:461-524 registers a NEW task-definition revision out-of-band on every deploy (`aws ecs register-task-definition` -> `aws ecs update-service --task-definition $NEW_TD_ARN`) with the image pinned to `@sha256:<digest>`. Terraform's own task definition is pinned to `${module.ecr.repository_urls["api"]}:${var.image_tag}` (terraform/environments/production/main.tf:118).

**Failure scenario.** Team deploys v1.4.0 through deploy-aws.yml; ECS service now points at task-def revision 41 (image digest for v1.4.0). A week later someone runs `terraform apply` to change `api_desired_count` or add a tag, passing the last-known `-var image_tag=sha-abc1234`. Terraform sees the service's `task_definition` differs from `aws_ecs_task_definition.api.arn` and issues `update-service` back to its own revision — silently rolling production from v1.4.0 to whatever `image_tag` was supplied, with no changelog entry and no deploy record. If the intervening release included a schema migration, the rolled-back code now runs against a forward-migrated schema.

**Fix.** Add `lifecycle { ignore_changes = [task_definition, desired_count] }` to both `aws_ecs_service.api` and `aws_ecs_service.worker`, and document that image rollout is owned exclusively by deploy-aws.yml.

#### `P0` DR-01 — No disaster recovery: zero alarms, zero backup plan, zero cross-region/cross-account copy, single NAT gateway, and documented DR commitments with no implementing code

`terraform/modules/rds/main.tf:33` · blocks **G2** · ~weeks · disaster-recovery

**Evidence.** `grep -rn 'aws_cloudwatch_metric_alarm|aws_sns_topic|aws_backup|aws_db_snapshot|replica|aws_flow_log|aws_wafv2|autoscaling' terraform/` returns ZERO matches across all 28 .tf files. RDS has `backup_retention_period = var.backup_retention_days` (35) and `multi_az = true` — in-region HA and PITR only. `aws_nat_gateway.this` is a single gateway in `aws_subnet.public[0]` (terraform/modules/vpc-secure/main.tf:62-67) and `aws_route_table.private` routes ALL private egress through it.

Against that, the repo makes specific commitments:
- docs/operations/audit-log-retention-policy.md:140-147: 'Daily Postgres logical backup with 35-day retention... Glacier archive is written to a separate AWS account with a one-way IAM trust so a compromise of the production account cannot delete the archive.' `grep -rniE 'glacier|lifecycle_configuration|cross.account' terraform/` -> zero matches.
- docs/SOP_KEY_MANAGEMENT.md:39-40: 'RPO: 0 (synchronous replication) / RTO: 1 hour'.
- docs/validation/PQ-CORTEX-001-PERFORMANCE_QUALIFICATION.md:462-464, 490-491: 'RTO <4 hours / RPO <15 minutes' with the actual-value cells still blank ('____h', '____min').
No postmortem, incident log, on-call rotation, PagerDuty/Opsgenie reference or restore-rehearsal record exists anywhere in docs/.

**Failure scenario.** us-east-1a suffers an AZ event. The single NAT gateway in public subnet [0] goes with it, so every private-subnet ECS task loses egress to Secrets Manager, ECR and the LLM providers — the API cannot pull secrets on task restart and cannot serve AI routes even though RDS multi-AZ failed over cleanly. Nobody is paged, because no CloudWatch alarm and no SNS topic exist. Separately, if a bad migration or an operator error corrupts the database, the team must attempt an RDS point-in-time restore they have never rehearsed, against a schema the app mutates at boot; there is no measured RTO to tell a customer, and the DPA in audit-log-retention-policy.md has already promised a Glacier cross-account archive that does not exist.

**Fix.** (1) Deploy a NAT gateway per AZ. (2) Add aws_cloudwatch_metric_alarm + aws_sns_topic for: ECS running-task count below desired, ALB 5xx rate, ALB unhealthy host count, RDS CPU/storage/connections, RDS failover event. (3) Add aws_backup vault + plan with cross-region copy, or automated cross-region snapshot copy. (4) Either build the cross-account Glacier archive audit-log-retention-policy.md promises or strike the claim before it reaches a DPA. (5) Run and document one real restore drill with a measured RTO/RPO and fill in the PQ-CORTEX-001 blanks.

#### `P0` TF-EVIDENCE-01 — The 21 CFR Part 11 evidence stack (CloudTrail + WORM bucket) cannot be applied — missing bucket policy, empty KMS key policy, and a KMS alias nothing creates

`terraform/modules/compliance-evidence/main.tf:96` · blocks **G3** · ~days · regulatory-infrastructure

**Evidence.** `aws_cloudtrail.part11_audit` writes to `aws_s3_bucket.evidence` (line 98) but the module defines NO `aws_s3_bucket_policy` for that bucket — CloudTrail requires an explicit bucket policy granting `cloudtrail.amazonaws.com` s3:GetBucketAcl + s3:PutObject.

`aws_kms_key.cloudtrail_encryption { policy = var.kms_policy }` (line 51-56); terraform/environments/production/main.tf:153 passes `kms_policy = ""`, and staging/variables.tf:41-44 defaults it to `""`. The module's own TODO at line 64-65 admits the key policy is not wired: 'ensure var.kms_policy grants logs.<region>.amazonaws.com kms:Encrypt*/Decrypt*/GenerateDataKey*'.

S3 SSE points at `kms_master_key_id = var.kms_key_id` (line 46); production main.tf:152 passes the literal alias `"alias/c2c-prod-evidence"` — no `aws_kms_alias` resource with that name exists anywhere in terraform/.

**Failure scenario.** An operator follows terraform/README.md:42-50 and runs `terraform apply` in environments/production. `aws_s3_bucket_server_side_encryption_configuration.evidence` fails with NotFoundException on alias/c2c-prod-evidence, and `aws_cloudtrail.part11_audit` fails with InsufficientS3BucketPolicyException (and, with an empty key policy, InsufficientEncryptionPolicyException). Because these are creation errors, the immutable-evidence bucket, the 7-year Object Lock and the multi-region audit trail that the RDS/VPC modules tag as `Part11Control` never come into existence. A Part 11 inspector asking 'show me the tamper-evident infrastructure evidence trail' is shown terraform that has never successfully run.

**Fix.** Add `aws_s3_bucket_policy.evidence` with the standard CloudTrail service-principal statements scoped to the trail ARN; replace `var.kms_policy` with a `data.aws_iam_policy_document` built in-module granting cloudtrail.amazonaws.com and logs.<region>.amazonaws.com the required key actions; create `aws_kms_alias` for the key and reference `aws_kms_key.cloudtrail_encryption.arn` for bucket SSE instead of a hand-typed alias string.

#### `P1` LINT-01 — The lint gate cannot fail — 6,273 warnings, 0 errors, exit 0 — and the one strict invocation swallows its exit code

`.github/workflows/pr-checks.yml:99` · blocks **G2** · ~days · ci-gate-integrity

**Evidence.** I ran the exact CI command on the current tree:
  `npx eslint .` -> 4,264 files linted, 1,455 with problems, **0 errors, 6,273 warnings**, exit 0.
  Top rules: @typescript-eslint/no-unused-vars=1679, complexity=1333, no-console=1065, max-lines-per-function=939, max-lines=371, max-depth=245.

`npm run lint` is `eslint .` (package.json) with no `--max-warnings`, and eslint.config.js sets nearly every rule to 'warn' (lines 175-260). It is invoked as a gate at .github/workflows/ci.yml:220-221 and .github/workflows/deploy-aws.yml:73-74.

The only strict invocation is neutered:
```
npx eslint $FILES --max-warnings 0 || echo "Lint warnings found"
```
(pr-checks.yml:99) — `|| echo` makes the step exit 0 regardless.

Coverage of the gate is further narrowed by eslint.config.js:34 (`'client/src/**'` — 871 files / ~191 KLOC excluded) and :46 (`'scripts/**'` — every CI checker excluded from linting).

**Failure scenario.** A PR introduces 400 new warnings, an unused variable that shadows a real one, and a 900-line function. `npm run lint` prints them and exits 0. ci.yml's 'Run ESLint' step is green, deploy-aws.yml's 'Run linter' pre-deploy gate is green, and pr-checks.yml prints 'Lint warnings found' and passes. No human is required to look. The gate is reported on the PR as a passing check, which is worse than having no gate — a reviewer reasonably reads green as 'lint clean'.

**Fix.** Set `lint` to `eslint . --max-warnings <current count>` and ratchet it down (the same baseline pattern this repo already uses for typecheck and tenant-isolation), remove the `|| echo` in pr-checks.yml:99, and either lint client/src or stop calling this a repository lint gate.

#### `P1` NIGHTLY-01 — Both nightly jobs fail deterministically and have for as long as the debt existed — the CI failure feed is not watched

`.github/workflows/ci.yml:825` · blocks **G2** · ~days · ci-gate-integrity

**Evidence.** The `nightly-governance` job runs two steps:
```
- name: Route mount audit (strict debt gate)
  run: npm run ci:audit-route-mounts:full-strict     # --max-warnings 0
- name: Repo health audit (strict debt gate)
  run: npm run audit:repo-health:full-strict         # --strict, no baseline
```
I executed both on the current tree:
  `node scripts/ci/audit-route-mounts.mjs --max-warnings 0 ...` -> EXIT=1 (8 errors, 7 warnings)
  `node scripts/audits/repo-health-scan.mjs --strict ...` -> EXIT=1 (249 duplicate basenames, 30 files over byte threshold, 84 over line threshold)
With no baseline, scripts/audits/repo-health-scan.mjs:383-390 fails whenever any of those counts is > 0, so this can never pass.

Separately, .github/workflows/nightly-contradiction-scan.yml:61 runs `python -m shadow_service.nightly_scan` daily at 03:00 UTC. `shadow_service/` contains only an empty nested directory; `find . -name 'nightly_scan*'` returns nothing. That job also mounts `secrets.DATABASE_URL` and `secrets.REVIEW_ADMIN_TOKEN` (lines 39-40).

**Failure scenario.** Every night at 03:00 and 06:00 UTC, GitHub Actions emails a failure for two scheduled workflows. Because they have failed continuously, the failures are indistinguishable from background noise, so a genuinely NEW nightly regression — e.g. a route-mount error class the PR-time no-regression gate lets through — produces no distinguishable signal. The same non-watching explains why nightly-contradiction-scan.yml has been executing a non-existent Python module against a secret DATABASE_URL for an unknown period.

**Fix.** Either delete the two permanently-red steps or convert them to baseline-relative gates that can pass today and ratchet. Delete nightly-contradiction-scan.yml or restore shadow_service/nightly_scan.py; in either case revoke the DATABASE_URL and REVIEW_ADMIN_TOKEN repository secrets it holds if the job is dead.

#### `P1` OBS-01 — On-call has no correlation ID: errorHandler reads req.id which is never assigned, and two independent request-ID generators disagree

`server/middleware/errorHandler.ts:141` · blocks **G1** · ~hours · observability

**Evidence.** errorHandler.ts logs and returns `request_id: (req as any).id` at lines 98, 120, 141 and 149. `grep -rn '(req as any)\.id = |req\.id = ' server/` returns NOTHING — `req.id` is never assigned anywhere in the repo. The actual property is `req.requestId`, set at server/middleware/enterprise-security.ts:805.

Worse, two generators coexist:
- enterprise-security.ts:802-807 `requestId()` validates a client-supplied `x-request-id` against `/^[A-Za-z0-9._-]{1,128}$/` and sets `req.requestId` + `res.setHeader('X-Request-Id', id)`. It is mounted first (line 957).
- server/src/mw/observability.ts:83 `httpLogger` does `const requestId = (req.headers['x-request-id'] as string) || randomUUID()` — it reads the REQUEST header (unset unless the client sent one), generates a different UUID, then OVERWRITES the response header at line 84. It is mounted later (server/startup/middleware.ts:66).

So the [AUDIT] line (enterprise-security.ts:675, `console.log('[AUDIT]', JSON.stringify(entry))`) carries ID-A, the access log (`request_failed`/`request_ok`, observability.ts:60-79) carries ID-B, the client receives ID-B, and every error log line carries `undefined`. httpLogger also applies no format validation to a client-supplied id, unlike the middleware that was written specifically to guard it.

**Failure scenario.** A pilot customer emails: 'I got Internal server error saving section 2.7 at 14:32 UTC.' The response body they screenshot has no request_id (the key is dropped because it is undefined). Support pulls CloudWatch for 14:32 and finds several `Server error in POST /api/...` lines, each with `context.request_id: undefined`, and cannot tell which one is the customer's. If the customer had captured the X-Request-Id response header, it matches the httpLogger access line but NOT the [AUDIT] line for the same request — so the Part 11-relevant audit record and the error record cannot be joined by ID at all.

**Fix.** Replace `(req as any).id` with `(req as any).requestId` in all four errorHandler sites; make httpLogger reuse `(req as any).requestId` instead of generating its own, and drop its unvalidated header read; stop setting X-Request-Id twice.

#### `P1` OBS-02 — OpenTelemetry is dead code — no @opentelemetry package is installed, and the loader degrades to a warning

`server/services/telemetry/opentelemetry.ts:69` · blocks **G2** · ~days · observability

**Evidence.** The initializer dynamic-imports three packages:
```
const [{ NodeSDK }, { getNodeAutoInstrumentations }, { OTLPTraceExporter }] = await Promise.all([
  dynamicImport('@opentelemetry/sdk-node'),
  dynamicImport('@opentelemetry/auto-instrumentations-node'),
  dynamicImport('@opentelemetry/exporter-trace-otlp-http'),
]);
```
and catches every failure with `logger.warn('initialization skipped', ...)` (lines 96-98). Filtering package.json dependencies + devDependencies for /opentelemetry/ returns ZERO packages (only @sentry/node, @sentry/react, prom-client match the observability filter). `initializeOpenTelemetry()` is awaited at server/index.ts:27.

The file's own header (lines 15-19) claims it replaced silent-drop behaviour: 'The silent-drop behavior this replaces was: misconfigured production boxes initialized the SDK with traceExporter: undefined and silently lost every trace.' It reintroduces exactly that, one level up.

SENTRY_DSN is likewise never provisioned: terraform/modules/ecs-fargate/main.tf:138-146 does not set it, and server/startup/env.ts:81-86 downgrades it to a `console.warn` 'Recommended env vars not set'.

**Failure scenario.** An operator sets OTEL_ENABLED=true and OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT=https://collector.internal, restarts, and sees `[OTel] enabled...` never appear — instead one `initialization skipped` warn line among thousands. No trace ever reaches the collector. `GET /api/ai-assistance` diagnostics (server/routes/ai-assistance.ts:408) would report `{enabled: true, started: false}`, but nothing alerts on it. In production, Sentry is also silent because SENTRY_DSN is unset in the terraform task definition, so the platform has neither distributed tracing nor error aggregation — only CloudWatch text.

**Fix.** Either add the three @opentelemetry packages as production dependencies and make a failed import in production a hard boot failure (matching the existing OTEL_ALLOW_NO_EXPORTER escape hatch pattern at lines 47-56), or delete the module and stop advertising tracing. Add SENTRY_DSN to terraform/modules/secrets and the api_secrets list.

#### `P1` ALERT-01 — Six alert rules for one subsystem, all six runbook links 404, and nothing on the ECS path ever loads them

`infra/alerts/orchestrator.yml:46` · blocks **G2** · ~weeks · alerting

**Evidence.** infra/alerts/orchestrator.yml is the only alert definition in the repository. Its six rules all target `submission_orchestrator_*` metrics. Four carry `runbook_url` (lines 46, 76, 104, 130) pointing at docs/runbooks/orchestrator-gateway-not-ready.md, orchestrator-sequence-query-failed.md, orchestrator-step-failure-rate.md and orchestrator-tenant-isolation.md. `ls docs/runbooks/orchestrator-*` -> 'No such file or directory'; docs/runbooks/ contains only corpus-ingestion.md, ectd-dtd-vendoring.md, env-var-documentation-gate.md, esg-production-setup.md and fda-esg-production-uat.md.

Nothing loads the file: `grep -rn 'infra/alerts' terraform/ charts/ .github/ docker-compose*.yml` returns only a source comment at server/startup/inline-endpoints.ts:190. Terraform contains no Prometheus, no AMP workspace and no ADOT collector. The metrics themselves are process-local in-memory counters rendered by /api/metrics (server/startup/inline-endpoints.ts:144-198), and terraform runs `api_desired_count = 2` behind an ALB, so a scrape hits one of two tasks at random and sees half the counts.

**Failure scenario.** A tenant_isolation_violation fires in the orchestrator — the repo itself calls every occurrence 'a 21 CFR Part 11 §11.10(e) incident' (orchestrator.yml, SubmissionOrchestratorTenantIsolationViolation). On the AWS deployment nobody is paged, because no Prometheus exists to evaluate the rule. If a k8s operator did load the rules, the pager fires with `runbook_url: docs/runbooks/orchestrator-tenant-isolation.md`, which is a dead link, and the on-call engineer has no triage procedure at 03:00. Every other subsystem — auth, billing, document export, the AI gateway, the database — has no alert at all.

**Fix.** Write the four missing runbooks. Decide the metrics substrate: if ECS is the target, ship an ADOT/CloudWatch-agent sidecar and translate these rules to CloudWatch alarms + SNS; if Prometheus, add the scrape target and rules_files wiring to terraform/charts. Move the per-process counters behind a shared store or accept and document that /api/metrics is per-task. Extend alert coverage beyond one subsystem to at minimum: ALB 5xx rate, ECS task count, RDS health, auth failure rate.

#### `P1` TRIVY-01 — deploy-aws.yml makes the Trivy IaC scan blocking, but the known HIGH finding it names is not in .trivyignore — the production security gate cannot pass

`.github/workflows/deploy-aws.yml:126` · blocks **G1** · ~hours · deployment-correctness

**Evidence.** Pre-deploy gate, no continue-on-error:
```
- name: Trivy config scan (Dockerfile/IaC)
  uses: aquasecurity/trivy-action@v0.36.0
  with: { scan-type: config, scan-ref: ., severity: CRITICAL,HIGH, exit-code: '1', skip-dirs: node_modules }
```
ci.yml:789-796 marks the identical scan `continue-on-error: true` and explains why: 'the IaC estate has a pre-existing backlog of HIGH/CRITICAL misconfigs (e.g. AVD-AWS-0164 public-IP-on-launch in modules/vpc-secure) that must be baselined before this can block.'

That finding is real — terraform/modules/vpc-secure/main.tf:18 `map_public_ip_on_launch = true` — and .trivyignore does NOT list AVD-AWS-0164. Its only AWS entries are AVD-AWS-0016, 0086, 0087, 0091, 0093 plus DS002.

The security-gate job is a hard `needs:` of build-push (deploy-aws.yml:139), which is a `needs:` of migrate, deploy-api and deploy-worker.

**Failure scenario.** A release engineer pushes tag v1.1.0. The security-gate job runs the config scan, Trivy reports AVD-AWS-0164 (HIGH) on terraform/modules/vpc-secure/main.tf:18, the step exits 1, security-gate fails, and build-push/migrate/deploy-api/deploy-worker are all skipped. Combined with DEPLOY-01, the production deploy path is blocked at two independent points — which is consistent with the observation that the repository carries exactly one tag (v1.0.0) and no deploy history.

**Fix.** Fix `map_public_ip_on_launch = true` (the public subnets carry only the ALB, which does not need it) rather than suppressing it, then re-run the scan and baseline whatever legitimately remains into .trivyignore with justifications, matching the discipline already used for CVE-2026-45829 and GHSA-qwww-vcr4-c8h2.

#### `P1` SMOKE-01 — Post-deploy smoke test probes the always-200 endpoint over --insecure TLS and silently passes when it cannot find the ALB

`.github/workflows/deploy-aws.yml:674` · blocks **G1** · ~hours · deployment-correctness

**Evidence.** ```
ALB_DNS=$(aws elbv2 describe-load-balancers --names c2c-prod --query 'LoadBalancers[0].DNSName' --output text 2>/dev/null || echo "")
if [ -n "$ALB_DNS" ]; then
  STATUS=$(curl -s -o /dev/null -w "%{http_code}" "https://$ALB_DNS/api/health" --insecure || true)
  ...
else
  echo "⚠️ Could not resolve ALB DNS — skipping health check"
fi
```
(lines 676-691). Three defects: it probes `/api/health`, which Dockerfile.optimized:76-78 documents as returning 200 against a missing-schema DB; it passes `--insecure`, disabling certificate verification against the production endpoint; and if `describe-load-balancers` fails or the name changes, the else-branch prints a warning and the job exits 0. The job condition is also `if: always() && (needs.deploy-api.result == 'success' || needs.deploy-frontend.result == 'success')` (line 665) — a frontend-only success is enough to run and pass it.

**Failure scenario.** A deploy ships code that reads a table the migration did not create. deploy-api succeeds (ECS reports healthy tasks — see HEALTH-01), the smoke test curls /api/health, gets a static 200, and prints '✅ API health check passed'. The release is declared good. The first signal of the outage is a customer ticket. Alternatively, an IAM permission change breaks describe-load-balancers; the smoke test prints a warning and passes, so a completely dead deployment reports green.

**Fix.** Probe `/readyz` and require HTTP 200 with `ready: true`; drop `--insecure` (the ALB has an ACM cert — use the real domain from var.domain_aliases); make an unresolvable ALB a hard failure, not a skip; and gate the job on `needs.deploy-api.result == 'success'` alone.

#### `P1` COV-01 — Coverage thresholds are declared at 70/60/70/70 and overridden to 0 with continue-on-error — coverage has never gated anything

`.github/workflows/ci.yml:375` · blocks **G3** · ~weeks · test-coverage

**Evidence.** vitest.config.ts:47-52 declares `thresholds: { lines: 70, branches: 60, functions: 70, statements: 70 }`.

ci.yml:368-380 overrides all four to zero AND marks the step non-blocking:
```
- name: Measure coverage
  continue-on-error: true
  run: NODE_OPTIONS="--max-old-space-size=4096" npx vitest run --config vitest.config.ts --coverage \
       --coverage.thresholds.lines=0 --coverage.thresholds.functions=0 \
       --coverage.thresholds.branches=0 --coverage.thresholds.statements=0
```
The job is named 'Coverage (advisory)' (line 315) and nothing consumes its artifact — `build` needs [lint, test, ana-readiness-tests, integration-tests, aios-audit-assets] (line 706), not coverage. The provider itself is installed transiently with `--no-save` (line 366), so coverage cannot even be reproduced by a developer running `npm run test:coverage`.

**Failure scenario.** A PR deletes the only test covering the e-signature manifest builder. The Test job passes (fewer tests, all green), the Coverage job records a lower number in an artifact nobody opens, and `continue-on-error` keeps it green. There is no number in the repository stating actual coverage, so a vendor-qualification auditor asking 'what is your test coverage of the Part 11 critical path, and what is your minimum?' has no answer. For G3 this is an IQ/OQ evidence gap, not merely a quality one.

**Fix.** Run the coverage job once, record the real numbers, set the thresholds to those numbers minus a small margin, remove `continue-on-error`, add @vitest/coverage-v8 as a real devDependency, and ratchet toward 70/60/70/70. Report per-directory coverage for the Part 11 modules separately.

#### `P1` DOC-DEPLOY-01 — The canonical deployment guide documents a different product from a Helm repo that is not this one

`docs/operations/DEPLOYMENT.md:33` · blocks **G1** · ~days · operability-documentation

**Evidence.** docs/operations/DEPLOYMENT.md:1-3 — 'TrialSage CER Generator Deployment Guide... deploying the TrialSage CER Generator system in various environments using Kubernetes and Helm.' Line 33: `helm repo add trialsage https://charts.trialsage.com`; lines 37-40 and 58-68 install `trialsage/trialsage-cer` with `trialsage/cer-api` images (charts/trialsage-cer/values.yaml:38-40). It never mentions AWS ECS, terraform, Dockerfile.optimized, deploy-aws.yml, or charts/concept2cure-app — the paths that actually deploy this application.

Adjacent dead deployment assets compound it: helm/values-local.yaml:4 supplies values for a chart named `ros-api` that does not exist in charts/; infra/k8s/bff-with-predicate-shadow.yaml:6-8 states 'This is a reference. The actual production manifests live in the deploy repository' — a repository not in this codebase; and .github/workflows/cerv2-staging-deploy.yml:15-22 offers a `staging-vercel` environment choice that no job step reads.

**Failure scenario.** A new SRE (or an acquirer's integration team) opens the only file in docs/operations named DEPLOYMENT.md, runs `helm repo add trialsage https://charts.trialsage.com`, and either gets a 404 or installs an entirely different service. Nothing in the doc tree points them at deploy-aws.yml or terraform/environments/production. Operator time is burned and, worse, the acquirer's diligence team may conclude a Kubernetes production path exists when the only real one is ECS.

**Fix.** Replace docs/operations/DEPLOYMENT.md with the actual ECS path (terraform bootstrap -> terraform apply -> tag push -> deploy-aws.yml -> rollback via prior task-def ARN) and a separate section for the charts/concept2cure-app self-host path. Delete helm/values-local.yaml, charts/trialsage-cer and the staging-vercel input, or move them under a clearly-labelled _archive/.

#### `P1` PART11-CI-01 — The 'Part 11 Validated' ALCOA+ evidence workflow cannot run — three of its inputs do not exist

`.github/workflows/ci-staging-integration.yml:81` · blocks **G3** · ~days · regulatory-infrastructure

**Evidence.** The workflow is named 'ROS Staging Integration (Part 11 Validated)' and its smoke-test job is 'Part 11 E2E Smoke Test'. It runs:
```
- name: Install Dependencies
  run: |
    pip install -r requirements-staging.txt
    pip install -e .
...
- run: python scripts/e2e_staging_smoke.py --environment ... --strict-part11
- run: python scripts/generate_part11_report.py --evidence-dir ./evidence --output ./evidence/ALCOA_plus_report.md
```
File existence check: requirements-staging.txt MISSING; setup.py MISSING; pyproject.toml MISSING (so `pip install -e .` has no package to install); scripts/generate_part11_report.py MISSING. Only scripts/e2e_staging_smoke.py exists.

**Failure scenario.** A quality lead is asked during a vendor qualification for the ALCOA+ evidence pack. They trigger the workflow; `pip install -r requirements-staging.txt` fails on line 1 of the Install Dependencies step. No evidence artifact, no ALCOA_plus_report.md, has ever been produced. Because the workflow is `workflow_dispatch` only and has presumably never been run, its permanent brokenness has never surfaced, and its existence in the repo reads to a diligence reviewer as evidence that Part 11 E2E validation is automated.

**Fix.** Either restore requirements-staging.txt + pyproject.toml + scripts/generate_part11_report.py and prove one green run whose artifacts are retained, or delete the workflow. A named-but-nonfunctional compliance workflow is worse than none in a GxP context.

#### `P1` RDS-AUDIT-01 — pgaudit is configured but never enabled — shared_preload_libraries is not set and the extension is never created

`terraform/modules/rds/main.tf:71` · blocks **G3** · ~hours · regulatory-infrastructure

**Evidence.** ```
parameter {
  name  = "pgaudit.log"
  value = "write,ddl"
  apply_method = "pending-reboot"
}
```
The parameter group (lines 52-78) sets log_connections, log_disconnections, log_statement=ddl and pgaudit.log, but never sets `shared_preload_libraries = "pgaudit"`, and no migration or provisioning script issues `CREATE EXTENSION pgaudit`. AWS requires both. The instance is nevertheless tagged `Part11Control = "DataIntegrity"` (line 46) and the module header (line 1) says '21 CFR Part 11 compliant'.

**Failure scenario.** During an inspection the team is asked to produce database-level write/DDL audit records independent of the application's own audit tables — the standard defence against 'the application wrote its own audit trail'. They point at the pgaudit parameter in terraform. Postgres has no pgaudit extension loaded, so `pgaudit.log` is an inert custom GUC and no pgAudit records exist for the entire retention window. The only remaining record is log_statement=ddl in the postgresql CloudWatch export, which captures DDL but no DML writes.

**Fix.** Add `parameter { name = "shared_preload_libraries" value = "pgaudit" apply_method = "pending-reboot" }` and run `CREATE EXTENSION pgaudit;` in the provisioning path (scripts/db/install-fresh.mjs), then verify with `SHOW shared_preload_libraries` and a probe write.

#### `P1` SEC-TF-01 — Terraform writes jwt_secret and openai_api_key in plaintext into remote state, and the README claims the opposite

`terraform/modules/secrets/main.tf:12` · blocks **G2** · ~days · secrets-handling

**Evidence.** ```
resource "aws_secretsmanager_secret_version" "this" {
  for_each      = var.secrets
  secret_id     = aws_secretsmanager_secret.this[each.key].id
  secret_string = each.value.value
}
```
Values come from `var.jwt_secret` / `var.openai_api_key` (terraform/environments/production/main.tf:59-69, variables.tf:90-98). Terraform persists every attribute, including `secret_string`, into the state file at s3://c2c-terraform-state/production/terraform.tfstate (production/main.tf:11-17).

terraform/README.md:54 asserts 'All secrets are stored in AWS Secrets Manager, never in tfvars' — while README.md:49, five lines earlier, instructs `terraform apply -var="jwt_secret=..." -var="openai_api_key=..."`. There is also no `aws_secretsmanager_secret_rotation` for either secret.

By contrast the RDS module does this correctly: `manage_master_user_password = true` (modules/rds/main.tf:21) keeps the DB password out of state entirely — proof the team knows the right pattern.

**Failure scenario.** A customer security questionnaire asks 'where are application signing keys stored and who can read them?' The honest answer is: in Secrets Manager AND in the terraform state object in S3, readable by every principal with s3:GetObject on the state bucket and by anyone who runs `terraform show` or `terraform state pull` locally. A contractor granted read access to the state bucket for a plan review obtains the production JWT signing secret, which — per server/config/environment.ts — signs access tokens for every tenant. The README's flat denial makes this a documentation-vs-code contradiction a reviewer will find.

**Fix.** Create the secrets with `aws_secretsmanager_secret` only (no `_version`), populate the values out-of-band (CLI/console/CI with OIDC), and add `lifecycle { ignore_changes = [secret_string] }` if a version resource must remain. Correct README.md:52-59. Add rotation for the OpenAI key at minimum.

#### `P1` NET-01 — VPC has no flow logs, the KMS interface endpoint is unreachable (SG has zero ingress), and there is no WAF or ALB access logging

`terraform/modules/vpc-secure/main.tf:99` · blocks **G2** · ~days · network-security

**Evidence.** ```
resource "aws_security_group" "endpoint" {
  name = "ros-part11-endpoint-sg"
  vpc_id = aws_vpc.this.id
  egress { from_port = 0, to_port = 0, protocol = "-1", cidr_blocks = [] }
  tags = var.tags
}
```
No `ingress` block at all — the KMS interface endpoint (lines 91-97) therefore rejects all inbound traffic from the private subnets, and the `cidr_blocks = []` egress removes the AWS default allow-all without replacing it. `aws_security_group.rds_part11` (lines 112-133) has the same empty-CIDR egress.

No `aws_flow_log` exists in the module or anywhere in terraform/ (`grep -rn aws_flow_log terraform/` -> zero). No `aws_wafv2_*` resource exists. `aws_lb.this.access_logs` is enabled only when `var.access_logs_bucket != ""` (modules/alb/main.tf:12-16) and terraform/environments/production/main.tf:91-101 never passes it, so ALB access logging is off in production. Public subnets set `map_public_ip_on_launch = true` (line 18).

**Failure scenario.** A customer reports suspected unauthorised access. Forensics needs to answer 'what source IPs reached the ALB and what did the ECS tasks talk to?' There are no VPC flow logs and no ALB access logs, so neither question can be answered for any period. Separately, the module's own KMS VPC endpoint is dead weight — every KMS call silently traverses the NAT gateway instead, so the network-boundary control the module is named for (`Part11Control = "NetworkBoundary"`) is not what is actually enforcing traffic.

**Fix.** Add `aws_flow_log` to CloudWatch or S3 with a retention that matches the audit policy; add an ingress rule on the endpoint SG allowing 443 from the private subnet CIDRs; replace the empty-CIDR egress blocks with explicit rules; pass an `access_logs_bucket` for the ALB; set `map_public_ip_on_launch = false`; add AWS WAF in front of the ALB or CloudFront.

#### `P2` CI-ARCH-01 — Every test job depends on a 35-step sequential Lint job, so one flaky guardrail skips the entire test matrix

`.github/workflows/ci.yml:236` · blocks **G2** · ~days · ci-architecture

**Evidence.** `test`, `coverage`, `ana-readiness-tests`, `integration-tests`, `blank-db-provisioning` and `aios-audit-assets` all declare `needs: lint`; `build` declares `needs: [lint, test, ana-readiness-tests, integration-tests, aios-audit-assets]` (line 706). The `lint` job runs ~35 sequential guardrail steps (lines 39-234), any one of which fails the job.

The workflow itself documents the consequence at lines 393-397: 'The other test jobs `needs: lint`, so they are skipped whenever the lint job is red (e.g. pre-existing tenant-isolation baseline drift). A security regression must always report, so this gate runs on its own.' — the fix was applied only to `security-tests`.

The fragility is structural: repo-health-baseline-refresh.yml exists solely because baseline drift in ONE guardrail was 'cascade-skipping Test/Build/Integration on every open PR' (its header, lines 6-11).

**Failure scenario.** A PR touches a migration and trips `ci:duplicate-table-ddl` (step 8 of 35). The lint job goes red. Test, Integration, Coverage, AnA Readiness, blank-db-provisioning and Build are all reported as *skipped*, not failed. GitHub branch protection treats a skipped required check as neutral in several configurations, so the PR can present as mergeable with zero tests having executed. Even where it blocks, the reviewer sees one red guardrail and five grey boxes and has no signal about whether the code works.

**Fix.** Split the guardrail suite into its own job (or a matrix of jobs) so lint failures do not gate test execution; make only `build` depend on both. Consider `if: always()` on the test jobs with an explicit aggregate gate job.

#### `P2` CI-ORPHAN-01 — check-env-var-docs.mjs maintains a 244-entry baseline for a gate with no npm script and no workflow invocation

`scripts/ci/check-env-var-docs.mjs:57` · blocks **G2** · ~hours · ci-gate-integrity

**Evidence.** The checker is fully implemented (313 lines, four modes, baseline at docs/reports/env-var-docs-baseline.json which currently holds `count`/`vars` with **244** entries). There is no `ci:env-var-docs` entry in package.json scripts, and `grep -rn 'check-env-var-docs' .github/ package.json .husky/` returns only self-references inside the script. docs/runbooks/env-var-documentation-gate.md documents the gate as if it runs.

I enumerated all 39 scripts/ci checkers against the workflow set: 32 run and block on every PR, 4 more run indirectly via check-reasoning-tier-readiness-suite.mjs, and 3 are orphaned — check-env-var-docs.mjs, report-branch-drift.mjs and generate-test-summary.js.

This matters directly to BOOT-01: the four boot-critical env vars are exactly the class this checker exists to surface.

**Failure scenario.** A developer adds `process.env.NEW_SIGNING_KEY` to a server module with a silent `|| ''` fallback and ships. Nothing documents it, nothing fails, and the terraform/helm/compose manifests are never updated — the same mechanism that produced BOOT-01. The 244-entry baseline sits in the repo giving the appearance of a managed ratchet while no run has ever consumed it.

**Fix.** Add `"ci:env-var-docs": "node scripts/ci/check-env-var-docs.mjs --strict-no-regression"` and a step in ci.yml's lint job; re-baseline; then extend the checker (or add a sibling) that cross-checks boot-required vars against terraform/modules/ecs-fargate, charts/concept2cure-app/values.yaml and docker-compose.yml.

#### `P2` BASELINE-01 — repo-health baseline is auto-regenerated and committed on every merge, so the debt gate can only ratchet upward

`.github/workflows/repo-health-baseline-refresh.yml:54` · ~days · ci-gate-integrity

**Evidence.** On every push to concept2cure-v2 the workflow regenerates and commits the baseline the PR gate compares against:
```
node scripts/audits/repo-health-scan.mjs --owners ... --write-markdown docs/reports/repo-health-scan-latest.md
...
git add docs/reports/repo-health-scan-latest.json docs/reports/repo-health-scan-latest.md
git commit -m "chore(repo-health): auto-refresh baseline after merge [skip ci]"
git push origin HEAD:concept2cure-v2
```
The PR gate reads the same path: `--strict-no-regression --baseline docs/reports/repo-health-scan-latest.json` (package.json audit:repo-health:no-regression). The workflow states the intent plainly at lines 12-14: 'PRs branched after a merge therefore always see delta 0.'

Current baseline: 249 duplicate basenames, 30 files over the byte threshold, 84 over the line threshold. (I confirmed the scan reads the baseline before overwriting it — scripts/audits/repo-health-scan.mjs:339 vs :369 — so within one run the comparison is honest.)

Related: the orphan-endpoint gate is set at `--threshold 600` (package.json:91, the `:strict` variant — package.json:90 is the non-strict script and takes no threshold) against 556 actual orphans (docs/reports/orphan-endpoints-latest.json) — 44 of headroom, ~8%. That headroom is deliberate and documented at `.github/workflows/ci.yml:202-205`; see the correction to item 0.8 in Chapter 15.

**Failure scenario.** Ten PRs each add three duplicate basenames. Each PR sees delta +3 against the baseline as of its branch point, fails, and the author bumps by re-basing after a merge that refreshed the baseline — or simply merges another PR first. Over a quarter the duplicate-basename count moves from 249 to 300 while every PR reported delta 0 and the gate reported green the whole time. There is no ceiling and no downward pressure; the only strict variant (`audit:repo-health:full-strict`) runs in the nightly job, which is permanently red (see NIGHTLY-01) and therefore ignored.

**Fix.** Make the baseline a hand-edited, reviewed file (like .typecheck-baseline.json, which was ratcheted to 0) rather than a machine-refreshed artifact, or add a monotonic ceiling that the refresh job refuses to exceed. Ratchet the orphan threshold to the actual count plus a small, justified margin.

#### `P2` SUPPLY-01 — Zero of 138 GitHub Actions references are SHA-pinned; Semgrep and the CI-side IaC scan are advisory

`.github/workflows/ci.yml:209` · blocks **G2** · ~days · supply-chain

**Evidence.** `grep -rhoP 'uses:\s*\K\S+' .github/workflows/ | wc -l` -> 140 (138 real references plus two grep artefacts); the count matching `@[0-9a-f]{40}$` is **0**. Every reference is a floating tag: actions/checkout@v4 (49x), actions/setup-node@v4 (30x), actions/upload-artifact@v4 (19x), aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials@v4 (7x), returntocorp/semgrep-action@v1, bridgecrewio/checkov-action@v12, neondatabase/create-branch-action@v6 and @v4, peter-evans/create-pull-request@v6, etc. The repo knows: terraform-compliance.yml:19-21 and :30-34 carry TODO(GA-blocker) comments about exactly this.

Adjacent advisory gates: semgrep.yml:32 sets `continue-on-error: true` at the JOB level, and its scan step ends with `exit 0` (line 56) so findings never fail; ci.yml:796 makes the Trivy IaC scan advisory. pr-checks.yml:22 additionally runs `returntocorp/semgrep-action@v1`, a deprecated action, in a second uncoordinated place.

**Failure scenario.** An attacker who compromises the `aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials` repo (or any maintainer account for the seven other third-party actions) re-points the `v4` tag at malicious code. On the next `deploy-aws.yml` run the action executes inside a job holding `id-token: write` and assuming `secrets.AWS_DEPLOY_ROLE_ARN` — full production AWS deploy credentials. The same tag mutation in `repo-health-baseline-refresh.yml` runs with `contents: write` on the default branch.

**Fix.** Pin all 138 `uses:` to 40-character commit SHAs with a trailing `# vX.Y.Z` comment and adopt Dependabot for actions. Replace the deprecated returntocorp/semgrep-action. Triage the Semgrep backlog and flip semgrep.yml to blocking on new findings (diff-aware `--baseline-commit`).

#### `P2` RC-01 — The RC pipeline calls a missing script, ignores its own environment input, and prints hard-coded pass marks

`.github/workflows/cerv2-staging-deploy.yml:279` · blocks **G2** · ~days · release-process

**Evidence.** Line 279 runs `node scripts/cerv2_staging_verify.mjs --base-url ... --ci`; that file does not exist in the repository.

The workflow_dispatch input `environment` offers `staging-docker` and `staging-vercel # Vercel preview deployment` (lines 17-22); no job step reads `inputs.environment` — the 'Gate 4: Staging Deploy' job always starts `node dist/index.js` on the CI runner (lines 253-271). Nothing is deployed anywhere despite the workflow name 'Staging Deploy + Production Verification'.

Gate 6 prints a fixed heredoc regardless of outcome (lines ~340-355): '1. Pre-flight ✔ Passed / 2. Build ✔ Passed / 3. Tests ✔ Passed' — while Gate 1's lint, typecheck and npm-audit steps are all `continue-on-error: true` (lines 118-129). It then force-pushes the RC tag: `git tag -f "$RC" || true; git push origin "$RC" --force || true`.

**Failure scenario.** A release manager selects `staging-vercel` and runs the workflow. Nothing is deployed to Vercel. The staging-deploy job fails at the CERV2 smoke step because the script is missing, so no RC tag is produced — or, if the smoke step is later stubbed, Gate 6 prints '✔ Passed' for a pre-flight in which lint, typecheck and `npm audit --audit-level=high` were all allowed to fail, and force-pushes an RC tag over any existing one, destroying release provenance.

**Fix.** Restore or delete scripts/cerv2_staging_verify.mjs; remove the unread `environment` input or implement it; replace the hard-coded summary with `${{ needs.<job>.result }}` values; drop `-f`/`--force` on the tag push.

#### `P2` HELM-01 — Helm migration init container points at a path the build never produces

`charts/concept2cure-app/values.yaml:108` · blocks **G1** · ~hours · deployment-correctness

**Evidence.** ```
migrations:
  enabled: false
  command: ['node', 'dist/scripts/migrate.js']
```
rendered as the init container command at charts/concept2cure-app/templates/deployment.yaml:30-43. `npm run build` is `vite build && node scripts/build-server.mjs`, and build-server.mjs emits a single `outfile: dist/index.js` (scripts/build-server.mjs:21) — `dist/` after a build contains only `index.js` and `public/`. The real migration entrypoint is `npm run db:migrate:deploy` -> scripts/db/deploy-migrate.mjs, which Dockerfile.optimized:62-64 deliberately copies into the image and which deploy-aws.yml:307 invokes correctly for ECS.

**Failure scenario.** A self-hosting customer follows the chart README, sets `migrations.enabled=true`, and installs. The init container runs `node dist/scripts/migrate.js`, exits with MODULE_NOT_FOUND, and the pod enters CrashLoopBackOff before the app container ever starts. The install appears to hang; the customer has no migration path and no error that names the actual entrypoint.

**Fix.** Change the default to `['npm', 'run', 'db:migrate:deploy']` and document the exit-3 unprovisioned-database contract that deploy-migrate.mjs enforces (the chart should point at scripts/db/install-fresh.mjs for first-time provisioning, which the production image intentionally cannot run).

#### `P2` LOG-01 — The redaction key list uses substring matching, so operational fields like tokenCount, sessionCount and graphId are logged as [REDACTED]

`server/utils/logger.ts:127` · ~hours · observability

**Evidence.** ```
const shouldRedact = SENSITIVE_KEYS.some(sensitive => lowerKey.includes(sensitive));
```
SENSITIVE_KEYS (lines 47-103) includes the bare substrings `'token'`, `'session'`, `'mfa'`, `'phi'` and `'secret'`. Because matching is `includes()` on the lowercased key, this redacts: `tokenCount`, `tokensUsed`, `promptTokens`, `completionTokens`, `totalTokens`; `sessionCount`, `sessionsActive`; and — because 'graphid'.includes('phi') is true — `graphId`, `graphIds`, `subgraphId`. The repo has an evidence-graph subsystem (cre_* tables) whose identifiers are exactly this shape.

Separately, server/middleware/enterprise-security.ts:675 writes the per-request security audit entry with `console.log('[AUDIT]', JSON.stringify(entry))` — the comment on line 674 says 'in production, this would go to a dedicated audit service'. That is one unstructured, unredacted line per request (carrying userId, organizationId and client IP) interleaved with the pino JSON stream, doubling log volume and breaking JSON-based log parsing.

**Failure scenario.** On-call investigates an LLM cost spike. Every log line the AI gateway emits shows `context.totalTokens: "[REDACTED]"`, `context.promptTokens: "[REDACTED]"`, so there is no way to attribute spend to a tenant or route from logs. Separately, tracing an evidence-graph write shows `context.graphId: "[REDACTED]"`, so the specific graph cannot be identified. Meanwhile a CloudWatch Logs Insights query over the structured stream silently drops every second line because the [AUDIT] lines are not valid JSON.

**Fix.** Match on exact key names plus a small set of anchored suffixes (`endsWith('_token')`, `endsWith('Token')`) rather than bare substrings; add an explicit allowlist for `tokenCount`/`totalTokens`/`promptTokens`/`completionTokens`/`graphId`. Route the [AUDIT] entry through the structured logger (or the real audit service) instead of console.log.

#### `P2` TF-NAME-01 — The compliance-evidence module hardcodes account-global 'ros-staging' names, so staging and production collide and production's Part 11 trail is named staging

`terraform/modules/compliance-evidence/main.tf:97` · blocks **G3** · ~hours · regulatory-infrastructure

**Evidence.** Three account-global names are hardcoded inside the module with no variable interpolation:
- line 61: `aws_cloudwatch_log_group.cloudtrail.name = "/aws/cloudtrail/ros-staging-part11-evidence"`
- line 71: `aws_iam_role.cloudtrail_cloudwatch.name = "ros-staging-part11-cloudtrail-cw"`
- line 97: `aws_cloudtrail.part11_audit.name = "ros-staging-part11-evidence"`

Both environments instantiate it: terraform/environments/staging/main.tf:35-43 and terraform/environments/production/main.tf:149-157.

**Failure scenario.** An operator applies staging (creating IAM role `ros-staging-part11-cloudtrail-cw` and trail `ros-staging-part11-evidence`) and then applies production in the same AWS account. `aws_iam_role.cloudtrail_cloudwatch` fails with EntityAlreadyExists and `aws_cloudtrail.part11_audit` with TrailAlreadyExistsException. If applied in the reverse order or in separate accounts, the PRODUCTION 21 CFR Part 11 evidence trail is literally named 'ros-staging-part11-evidence' — an inspector reading the CloudTrail console sees a staging-named trail asserted as the production evidence record.

**Fix.** Add a `name_prefix` (or reuse the existing `bucket_name`) variable and interpolate it into all three resource names, as the ecs-fargate and alb modules already do with `var.cluster_name` / `var.name`.

#### `P2` TF-CI-01 — Only the staging stack is validated in CI; the entire production terraform (RDS/ECS/ALB/CloudFront/secrets) is never planned or validated

`.github/workflows/terraform-compliance.yml:42` · blocks **G2** · ~days · ci-gate-integrity

**Evidence.** ```
- name: Terraform Plan (Read-only)
  working-directory: terraform/environments/staging
  run: |
    terraform init -backend=false
    terraform validate
    terraform plan -no-color -out=tfplan
```
staging/main.tf instantiates only `modules/vpc-secure` and `modules/compliance-evidence`. The production stack — ecr, secrets, rds, alb, ecs-fargate, cloudfront — is never validated, planned or Checkov-gated beyond the directory-wide Checkov step. The job also declares `id-token: write` but never runs `aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials`, so `terraform plan` has no AWS credentials.

The PR-comment step (lines 49-60) reads `tfplan` with `fs.readFileSync(..., 'utf8')` — a binary plan file — into a variable it then never uses, posting a fixed string.

This is the reason TF-EVIDENCE-01, BOOT-01 and TF-DRIFT-01 could all land: nothing ever ran `terraform validate` against environments/production.

**Failure scenario.** A PR adds a syntactically valid but semantically broken resource to modules/rds (or, as already happened, an ECS task definition missing four required env vars and an evidence module with no bucket policy). Checkov may or may not flag it; terraform never plans it. CI is green. The defect is discovered the first time someone attempts a production apply — which, given the evidence, has not yet happened.

**Fix.** Add a matrix over both environments running `terraform init -backend=false && terraform validate && terraform fmt -check`; add real OIDC credentials plus `terraform plan -refresh=false` against production with a read-only role; delete the dead readFileSync in the comment step.

#### `P2` DEPLOY-FE-01 — Frontend deploy does not depend on the migration job, so a blocked API rollout still ships new frontend code to users

`.github/workflows/deploy-aws.yml:613` · blocks **G1** · ~hours · deployment-correctness

**Evidence.** `deploy-frontend` declares `needs: [test, security-gate]` (line 613) whereas `deploy-api` and `deploy-worker` declare `needs: [build-push, migrate]` (lines 445, 537). The migrate job's own header (lines 226-244) explains that migrate is a hard gate 'so a failed or halted migration blocks the rollout instead of shipping code onto a schema that cannot serve it' — but the frontend bypasses it entirely, syncing `dist/public/` to S3 with `--delete` and invalidating CloudFront `/*` (lines 641-658).

**Failure scenario.** A release adds a new document-export screen plus the migration that creates its table. The migration task fails (exit non-zero); deploy-api and deploy-worker are correctly blocked, so the API stays on the previous release. deploy-frontend, unblocked, has already run `aws s3 sync --delete` and invalidated CloudFront. Every user now loads the NEW frontend against the OLD API: the new screen calls endpoints that do not exist and renders errors, and `--delete` has removed the previous build's hashed assets so a rollback requires a rebuild.

**Fix.** Add `migrate` (and ideally `deploy-api`) to `deploy-frontend`'s `needs:`, or split frontend releases behind a feature flag with an explicit compatibility contract. Stop using `--delete` on the same run that invalidates, or keep N-1 build assets.

### file handling, document generation & governed export

This unit is two codebases wearing one name. The eCTD/submission spine (leaf-source-resolver, assemble-from-core, submission-service transmit) is genuinely good work — tenant-scoped queries, %PDF magic verification on staged bytes, explicit "unresolved leaf" records instead of silent drops, and a transmit path that fails closed rather than ship an incomplete dossier. Around it sits a much older, much looser layer: a flat org-agnostic `generated_documents/` directory that any authenticated user of any tenant can read by filename and that silently overwrites on title collision; a `POST /api/mdx/imports` that reads any server path and JSZip-inflates every entry with no size, count, or ratio cap; and five AnA document tools that `fs.readFile` a model-chosen path even though the workspace guard written specifically to stop that (`document-workspace.ts`, whose header names `input_docx_path` as the motivating case) is wired into exactly one of six call sites. The dominant risk is that "governed" is a label, not a mechanism: the governed-export consequence hashes the client-posted editor JSON and never the bytes it hands back, the human-review gate is a boolean the client sets in its own request body, `regulatory_audit_logs` has no hash chain, and the Part 11 provenance rows are stamped with a literal `'127.0.0.1'`. The CI gates that are supposed to hold this line are substring greps that pass unchanged with every one of those defects present. Determinism is claimed but unimplemented: the "canonical" PDF converter has zero production callers and its determinism routine does variable-length in-place byte edits without repairing the xref table, tested only against a five-line synthetic fragment.

**Genuine strengths in this unit**

- Default-deny /api authentication boundary, mounted once before any route registration and enforcing in production — server/middleware/authBoundary.ts:145-195 and server/startup/middleware.ts:113-119. Its PUBLIC_API_ALLOWLIST (authBoundary.ts:58-103) documents a reason and an alternative control for every one of its 20 entries, and the prefix matcher (:111-117) deliberately prevents /api/healthxyz riding on /api/health. This is the single most valuable control in the unit: it is why every finding above requires an authenticated account rather than being open to the internet.
- eCTD transmit fails CLOSED on any unresolved leaf rather than shipping a silently incomplete dossier — server/services/submission-service/submission-service.ts:519-546. The comment reasons it out correctly ('There is no "external reference" in an eCTD backbone — a leaf resolves to a file inside the sequence'), releases the staged bundle, and throws DISPATCH_BLOCKED naming each missing leaf. Refusing to transmit is the rare and correct call here.
- server/services/ectd/leaf-source-resolver.ts is the best-engineered file in the unit: every document lookup is filtered by organizationId (:189, :206, :237), staged upload bytes are verified against the %PDF magic number and not the DB mime string (:50-52, :264-267, with the rationale written down at :44-48), non-materializable leaves become explicit UnresolvedLeaf records instead of silent drops, and vault_documents is deliberately left unresolved with a written explanation of the integer-vs-UUID and missing-org-column reasons (:62-73) rather than forced.
- server/middleware/uploadSafety.ts is a well-designed composite gate — magic-byte verification plus AV, with a production fail-closed policy on scanner unavailability (:151-168) and a documented dev/CI exception. Every one of its seven adoption sites unlinks the persisted temp file on rejection: analytics-routes.ts:78, protocol_routes.ts:710 and :770, api/templates/routes.ts:446, ctd-onboarding.ts:182, evidence-management.routes.ts:169 (all files in the batch), medical-device-routes.js:359-363, document-routes.ts:199-205. Consistency at the adoption sites is genuinely good; the problem is the number of sites, not their quality.
- server/utils/virusScan.ts is a 130-line dependency-free clamd INSTREAM client with bounded connect (5s) and scan (30s) timeouts, correct chunk framing, and — importantly — an honest three-way result that separates `scanned` from `clean` (:51-60), with the docstring explicitly warning 'Callers must NOT treat scanned:false as a clean signal'. It is backed by a live EICAR round-trip probe in the security health panel (server/services/securityHealth.ts:231).
- Tenant-scoped document download done right, with the reasoning recorded — server/routes/document-routes.ts:296-312: 'Tenant-scoped fetch — a foreign-tenant document is "not found". Without this, a download URL with a guessed id could serve another tenant's file content', and it audits BEFORE streaming bytes (:314-317) because 'the user requested it, that's the event'. server/routes/ivdr-binder-routes.ts:922-932 shows the same correct pattern for vault artifacts: resolve the version id from a row already filtered on organization_id.
- The classic multer filename-traversal class is closed at the parser layer across all ~29 upload sites: busboy applies its own basename() to the Content-Disposition filename by default (node_modules/busboy/lib/types/multipart.js:321-322, node_modules/busboy/lib/utils.js:479-491, which also maps '..' and '.' to ''), and no call site in the repo sets preservePath. This matters because six disk-storage sites do interpolate file.originalname straight into the on-disk name (DocumentDataCenterService.ts:197, ctd-onboarding.ts:41, api/validation/index.js:28 among them) and would otherwise be arbitrary-write primitives.
