The only thing it actually scrubs is plain objects, via `redactContext` (server/utils/logger.ts:119-137), which matches on KEY NAMES only and never inspects values. So the named scenario is not covered by either layer. The repo's own sanctioned error helper compounds it: server/utils/sendInternalError.ts:45-47 does `logger.error(userMessage, { err: rawMessage })` — `err` is not in SENSITIVE_KEYS (logger.ts:47-103), so raw third-party error text is logged by value with no scrubbing. The value-based scrubber that would catch this does exist (server/services/observability/redaction.ts:1-5, `redactSecretsAndPiiText`) and is correctly wired into the Sentry path (server/utils/sentry.ts:34) — it is simply not wired into stdout. Its SECRET_PATTERNS also lack coverage for connection-string credentials (`://user:pass@`) and AWS access key ids.

**Failure scenario.** I could not construct a live leak: I swept every `console.*` and `logger.*` call site for credential-valued arguments and found none that pass a secret today, so this is a defense-in-depth gap rather than a demonstrated disclosure. The concrete defect is that the control's stated contract is false. Any future or third-party error whose `message` embeds a credential — an SDK that stringifies its request config, a driver echoing a DSN — reaches stdout and the log shipper verbatim, and a reviewer reading consoleBridge.ts:11-15 would reasonably conclude it could not.

**Fix.** In `redactArgs`, run string arguments through `redactSecretsAndPiiText` and, for Error instances, scrub `message` and `stack` into a copy rather than passing the original through. Add `/[a-z][a-z0-9+.-]*:\/\/[^\s:@\/]+:[^\s@\/]+@/gi` and `/\bAKIA[0-9A-Z]{16}\b/g` to SECRET_PATTERNS. Either fix the docblock or fix the code — right now they disagree.

### Supply chain and runtime exposure (dependency posture, GitHub Actions pinning, container/port/DoS surface, webhook signature verification)

The dependency-hygiene *paperwork* on this repo is unusually good — the npm-audit allowlist has exactly one entry, its claim re-verifies (`npm ls esbuild --all` still shows 0.18.20/0.19.12/0.25.12 in-range, all under devDependencies that `npm ci --omit=dev` prunes), and the .trivyignore entries carry real reasoning. On the specific question the buyer asked: **the chromadb CVE-2026-45829 suppression's unrecorded determination is factually correct, and I established it three independent ways** — no Python file in the repo imports chromadb, `startPythonBackend()` is a stub returning `Promise.resolve(null)` so no Python process is ever spawned, and `Dockerfile.optimized` contains zero `pip install` lines. chromadb is not merely unreachable; it is never installed in the deployed artifact. The pin is dead code, though one documented manual-deploy path still installs it onto operator hosts.

The real risk in this area is not the dependency graph — it is the **runtime request-handling boundary**, where three defects are individually exploitable and empirically reproducible. I built and ran three repros against the repo's own installed express/express-rate-limit/stripe versions: (1) the express-rate-limit `keyGenerator` reads the raw `X-Forwarded-For` header with `validate: false`, and 40/40 login attempts with rotated XFF returned 401 instead of the expected 429 — every per-path throttle is fully bypassable; (2) a 48 KB gzip body to `/api/concept2cure` inflates to 50 MB and costs 182 MB RSS *before* the auth boundary rejects it with 401, a 1028x unauthenticated amplification against a 2 GB ECS task; (3) the Stripe webhook is mounted after the global JSON parser, so `constructEvent` receives a parsed object and returns "Webhook payload must be provided as a string or a Buffer" — the endpoint has never successfully processed a Stripe event, meaning cancellations and payment failures never revoke entitlements. Underlying two of these is a single omission: `app.set('trust proxy', …)` is never called anywhere in the bootstrap, which `server/routes/charters.ts:77-80` explicitly documents as a requirement the bootstrap MUST satisfy.

Actions posture: 0 of 140 `uses:` references are SHA-pinned, and the one job that assumes production AWS credentials (`build-push`) is the only deploy job *without* the `environment: production` approval gate. Compensating controls are real and I confirmed them rather than assuming: no `pull_request_target` anywhere, per-account lockout (5/30min) wired into both login paths, default-deny `/api` boundary with a reason-annotated allowlist, metrics/health-full behind `requireMetricsAuth`, and correct AWS security-group scoping.

**Genuine strengths in this unit**

- Default-deny /api authentication boundary is genuinely well-built, not a checkbox: mounted once at server/index.ts:123 before ANY route registration, defaults to 'enforce' in production (server/middleware/authBoundary.ts:124-128), delegates to the canonical authenticateToken rather than forking it, and its PUBLIC_API_ALLOWLIST (lines 58-103) has 16 entries where every single one carries a written reason AND names the alternative control covering it. The module docstring at lines 4-10 correctly diagnoses the prior failure mode it was built to fix. /api/shadow/health is conspicuously absent from the allowlist and is therefore protected — evidence the list was curated rather than copied.
- The npm-audit allowlist (scripts/ci/audit-with-allowlist.mjs:72-97) is the most rigorous artifact of its kind I have reviewed: exactly one entry, a written 'KEPT DESPITE BEING UNFLAGGED' paragraph explaining that npm audit has stopped reporting the advisory and that dropping it on a quiet scan would be the exact mistake the file warns against, and a falsifiable verification instruction. I ran that instruction — `npm ls esbuild --all` — and it confirms the claim exactly: 0.18.20, 0.19.12 and 0.25.12 are still installed and in-range. I further verified the 'build-time only' characterization holds: esbuild, vite, drizzle-kit and tsx are all devDependencies, and Dockerfile.optimized runs `npm ci --omit=dev`, so none reach the production image.
- No workflow anywhere uses `pull_request_target` (grepped across all 21 workflows). The entire pwn-request class — the highest-frequency real-world Actions compromise — is absent by construction rather than by luck.
- Production deploys cannot skip CI security gates. deploy-aws.yml is tag-triggered and so bypasses the PR-time checks, and the authors saw that: a dedicated `security-gate` job (lines 84-133) re-runs SAML fail-closed, dev-auth-in-prod, password hygiene, tenant-isolation and prompt-injection contract tests, npm audit, and blocking Trivy fs+config scans, and is a hard `needs:` of build-push (line 139). The rationale is written into the workflow at lines 79-83.
- Container images are deployed by content digest, not tag. deploy-aws.yml:169-200 captures the sha256 digest at push time and pins the ECS task definition to `<repo>@sha256:<digest>`, with mutable `:latest` deliberately removed and the reasoning recorded inline. terraform/environments/production/variables.tf goes further and refuses a default for image_tag so a floating tag can never be silently deployed.
- AWS network posture is correct and minimal: ECS task security group admits ingress only from the ALB security group on the container port (terraform/modules/ecs-fargate/main.tf:99-105), and the ALB admits only 80 and 443 from the internet with egress restricted to the VPC CIDR (terraform/modules/alb/main.tf:85-107). Nothing in the AWS path is over-exposed.
- Sensitive observability is properly gated. /api/metrics and /api/health/full sit behind requireMetricsAuth (server/startup/inline-endpoints.ts:39-55, 128, 144), which accepts either a valid JWT or a METRICS_TOKEN shared secret so Prometheus can scrape without a user session, and falls back to JWT-only when the token is unset — the right shape, and the token path is checked first for cost.
- Error messages are deliberately not echoed on public surfaces, with the reasoning written down in both places: the health checker's exception is logged server-side because 'messages from the health checker can carry DB DSN fragments, file paths, env var names' (inline-endpoints.ts:135-141), and the Stripe SDK's verification error is suppressed because echoing it 'fingerprints our implementation for whoever is probing the endpoint' (server/routes/billing.ts:283-286).
- The firecrawl HMAC comparison itself is cryptographically correct — constant-time via crypto.timingSafeEqual with a mandatory length pre-check to avoid the throw (server/integrations/firecrawl/client.ts:61-68) — and fails closed by returning false when FIRECRAWL_WEBHOOK_SECRET is unset (server/integrations/firecrawl/webhook.ts:5). Stripe uses the SDK's constructEvent rather than a hand-rolled HMAC, which brings the 300-second timestamp tolerance and therefore replay protection for free.
- The rate limiter fails CLOSED on the categories where availability is the lesser risk — auth (brute-force surface) and documents (exfiltration surface) — while other categories fail open, with the trade-off argued explicitly in server/middleware/redisRateLimiter.ts and server/config/platform-limits.ts:109-114. The sliding-window implementation also correctly un-counts rejected requests so a client at its limit cannot lock itself out indefinitely.
- Per-account lockout is real and wired in, not aspirational: 5 failed attempts triggers a 30-minute lock (server/services/auth-security-service.ts:26-27), enforced on both login paths (server/routes/auth.ts:427, server/routes/authEnterprise.ts:316), with the 423 response deliberately withholding the exact lockout timestamp to avoid leaking it (auth.ts:401-408).
- The AI gateway is a genuine policy chokepoint rather than a passthrough: role-aware prompt-injection scanning defaults to scanning ALL roles because 'hostile directives smuggled into ingested regulatory documents / RAG chunks / tool outputs are this platform's primary realistic vector' (policy.ts scanAllRolesEnabled), PII/PHI screening defaults ON with unknown detector labels failing closed, and — unusually honest — base64 document blocks that cannot be regex-classified are recorded as an `unscannable_document_block` coverage gap in the audit trail instead of being silently treated as clean (policy.ts:282-293).
- The prototype-pollution scrub was moved to AFTER the body parsers (server/startup/middleware.ts:77-82) with the bug it fixes spelled out — mounting it earlier made the body-side scrub a silent no-op. That is the exact class of ordering bug this review found elsewhere, caught and documented here.
- 21 CFR Part 11 immutability is enforced structurally: DELETE and bulk-delete are refused across five audit and e-signature route prefixes (server/startup/middleware.ts:144-180), the scope predicate is pure and unit-tested, and the comment notes none of those prefixes registers a destructive handler today — the guard exists to prevent a future regression rather than to patch a present bug.
- Dockerfile.optimized creates and switches to a non-root user, and its HEALTHCHECK deliberately probes /readyz rather than /api/health with the reason written inline: '/api/health is a static liveness ping that returns 200 even against a missing-schema DB, whereas /readyz fails closed on a down database or a positively-verified missing schema.'

#### `P1` SC-01 — Every per-path rate limit is bypassable: keyGenerator trusts raw X-Forwarded-For with express-rate-limit validation disabled

`server/middleware/enterprise-security.ts:384` · blocks **G1** · ~hours

**Evidence.** createLimiter() sets `validate: false` (line 384) — explicitly disabling express-rate-limit v8's built-in proxy-misconfiguration guard — and then supplies a keyGenerator (lines 386-392) that reads the raw header: `const forwarded = req.headers['x-forwarded-for']; const ip = Array.isArray(forwarded) ? forwarded[0] : forwarded?.split(',')[0] || req.ip; return ip || 'unknown';`. Because `app.set('trust proxy', …)` is never called anywhere in the bootstrap (grepped repo-wide: only hits are a test at server/__tests__/security/scim-ip-allowlist-enforcement.test.ts:46 and the doc comment at server/routes/charters.ts:77-80), Express performs no validation of this header, and an AWS ALB APPENDS to X-Forwarded-For rather than replacing it, so the attacker-supplied leftmost value survives to the app. These limiters guard /api/auth (5 req/15min prod), /api/ai (20/min), /api/export (10/min), /api/upload (20/min), /api/workflow and /api/documents (100/min) — mounted at enterprise-security.ts:985-990. I reproduced this against the repo's own express-rate-limit 8.5.1 with the createLimiter body copied verbatim: 8 attempts with no XFF gave `401 401 401 401 401 429 429 429` (throttle engages at 5, as configured); 40 attempts with a rotating `X-Forwarded-For: 10.9.x.y` gave 40x 401 and **0 of 40** 429s.

**Failure scenario.** Compensating control found and verified: per-account lockout exists (LOCKOUT_THRESHOLD=5, LOCKOUT_DURATION_MINUTES=30 at server/services/auth-security-service.ts:26-27) and IS wired into both login paths (server/routes/auth.ts:427, server/routes/authEnterprise.ts:316), so single-account brute force is capped. That control does NOT cover the attacks the IP throttle exists to stop. Concrete exploit — sustained platform-wide login denial: the attacker enumerates customer email addresses (trivially guessable in B2B pharma: firstname.lastname@customer.com) and sends `POST /api/auth/login {"email":"<victim>","password":"x"}` five times per address, each request carrying a fresh `X-Forwarded-For: <random IP>`. No 429 is ever returned. Every targeted account is locked for 30 minutes and can be re-locked indefinitely at a cost of 5 requests per account per half hour. Regulatory-affairs staff cannot log in to a submission deadline. Secondarily: password spraying (one common password across thousands of accounts) never trips the per-account counter and is now completely unthrottled, and /api/ai, /api/export and /api/upload lose their per-path throttles entirely for any authenticated user.

**Fix.** Two changes, both required. (1) Call `app.set('trust proxy', <exact hop count>)` in server/index.ts before applyCoreMiddleware — a literal integer matching the topology (1 for a single ALB), never bare `true`, which would re-open the spoof. (2) Delete the custom keyGenerator at enterprise-security.ts:386-392 and the `validate: false` at line 384 so express-rate-limit uses its own `req.ip`-based keyGenerator and its proxy-misconfiguration validator fires if trust proxy is ever wrong. Add a regression test asserting that 6 requests with distinct X-Forwarded-For values to /api/auth/login yield a 429.

#### `P1` SC-02 — trust proxy never set: all Redis rate-limit buckets collapse to the ALB address, giving a 20-request platform-wide login outage

`server/middleware/redisRateLimiter.ts:362` · blocks **G1** · ~hours

**Evidence.** getRateLimitKey() (lines 348-366) keys on `req.userId` when present, else falls back to `req.ip || (req.headers['x-forwarded-for'] as string)?.split(',')[0]?.trim() || 'unknown'` (line 362). Two facts make this collapse to a handful of global buckets in production. First, `req.userId` is never populated at this point: the limiter is mounted at server/startup/middleware.ts:60, the only assignment `req.userId = req.user.id` lives in server/middleware/tenantContext.ts:269 which is mounted per-router (never globally), and the auth boundary itself runs later at server/index.ts:123 — so the key is ALWAYS the IP branch, for authenticated and unauthenticated traffic alike. Second, with no `app.set('trust proxy')` anywhere, `req.ip` is the socket peer, which under ECS awsvpc behind the ALB (terraform/modules/ecs-fargate/main.tf:99-105 admits ingress only from the ALB security group) is the ALB's per-AZ ENI address. server/routes/charters.ts:77-80 documents exactly this hazard as a contract the bootstrap MUST satisfy: 'Behind a reverse proxy / ALB / nginx without trust proxy set, every row will pin to the proxy address… The bootstrap MUST set trust proxy for the deployment topology.' It does not. The production auth bucket is 20 requests / 15 minutes (server/config/platform-limits.ts:55-63, IS_PRODUCTION branch), and getCategory('/auth/login') routes there (redisRateLimiter.ts hasSegment on 'auth').

**Failure scenario.** An attacker with one host sends 20 `POST /api/auth/login` requests with garbage credentials — roughly 1.3 requests per minute. The key is `auth:ip:<ALB-ENI-address>`, shared by every user of every tenant. For the remainder of the 15-minute window every login, signup, password-reset, MFA and SAML/SSO callback on the platform returns HTTP 429 'Too many authentication attempts.' Repeating indefinitely costs the attacker ~2 requests/minute and is indistinguishable from ordinary traffic in logs. The same collapse applies to the 'api' bucket (100/min) and 'concept2cure' bucket (100/min) shared across all tenants, so any single busy customer starves every other customer. Two further consequences of the same root cause: the per-org SCIM source-IP allowlist (server/routes/scim.ts:182 clientIpOf → req.ip, used at :265) can never match a customer-supplied CIDR because it sees only the ALB address, so any customer who enables that control locks out their own IdP provisioning; and 21 CFR Part 11 e-signature records store `(req.headers['x-forwarded-for'])?.split(',')[0]` directly (server/routes/esignature.ts:269-272), letting a signer forge the IP address recorded against their own legally-binding signature by sending `X-Forwarded-For: 8.8.8.8`.

**Fix.** Set `app.set('trust proxy', 1)` (exact hop count for the ALB topology) in server/index.ts before applyCoreMiddleware. Then remove the raw-header fallbacks that become redundant and unsafe: redisRateLimiter.ts:362 should read `req.ip` only, and esignature.ts:269-272 plus the other 14 call sites that splice X-Forwarded-For by hand should use `req.ip`. Add a boot-time assertion that fails closed in production if trust proxy is unset, since three separate security controls silently depend on it.

#### `P1` SC-03 — Unauthenticated 1028x request-body decompression amplification on /api/concept2cure — 48 KB request costs 182 MB of a 2 GB task

`server/startup/middleware.ts:73` · blocks **G1** · ~hours

**Evidence.** `app.use('/api/concept2cure', express.json({ limit: '50mb' }))` is mounted inside applyCoreMiddleware, which server/index.ts:113 invokes ten lines BEFORE applyAuthBoundary at server/index.ts:123. The 50 MB body is therefore fully read, gzip-inflated and JSON.parse'd for every request to /api/concept2cure/* including unauthenticated ones, and only then does the boundary return 401. body-parser inflates `Content-Encoding: gzip` request bodies by default and enforces `limit` against the DECOMPRESSED byte count, so the wire cost to the attacker is the compressed size. The routes themselves are correctly guarded (server/bootstrap/register-concept2cure-routes.ts:11-15 applies authenticateToken) — the exposure is entirely the parser mounted ahead of the boundary. I reproduced this with the repo's own express 5.2.1: a 48,957-byte gzip body sent to /api/concept2cure/anything inflated to 50,331,658 bytes (1028x), took 610 ms of server time, grew RSS by 182 MB, and returned 401. Production ECS tasks default to 2048 MB (terraform/modules/ecs-fargate/variables.tf:45) with api_desired_count 2 (terraform/environments/production/variables.tf).

**Failure scenario.** An attacker who has never authenticated sends ~11 concurrent `POST /api/concept2cure/x` requests, each with `Content-Type: application/json`, `Content-Encoding: gzip`, and a 48 KB gzip of a 48 MB JSON string — about 540 KB of total upload. Each costs the task ~182 MB, so the 2 GB ECS task exhausts memory and is OOM-killed and replaced. Roughly 1 MB of upload across both tasks takes the entire production API offline, and it can be repeated on a loop. The Redis limiter IS mounted first (server/startup/middleware.ts:60) and is a genuine partial control, but its concept2cure bucket is 100 requests/minute (server/config/platform-limits.ts:88-92) — nine times more than needed to OOM a task — and per SC-02 that bucket is shared platform-wide rather than per-attacker, so it throttles the victims, not the attack.

**Fix.** Move the body parsers so they run AFTER applyAuthBoundary — an unauthenticated request should never allocate 50 MB. Additionally pass `inflate: false` on the 50 MB parser (base64 document bodies do not need transport compression on top of a JSON parser) or cap it via a `verify` callback that rejects on `req.headers['content-length']` exceeding a compressed-size ceiling, which removes the amplification entirely. Long term, move base64 document ingestion off the JSON parser onto a streaming multipart/presigned-upload path so no 50 MB parser exists on the /api surface at all.

#### `P1` SC-04 — Stripe webhook signature verification is structurally impossible — the endpoint has never processed an event, so cancellations never revoke entitlements

`server/routes/billing.ts:269` · blocks **G1** · ~hours

**Evidence.** The handler is `router.post('/webhooks/stripe', raw({ type: 'application/json' }), …)` and calls `stripe.webhooks.constructEvent(req.body, sig, webhookSecret)` at line 286. The file's own comment at lines 266-268 states the precondition: 'IMPORTANT: This must use raw body parsing for signature verification. It should be mounted BEFORE the JSON body parser in index.ts.' That precondition is violated. The router is mounted at server/bootstrap/register-inline-routes.ts:159 (`{ path: '/api/billing', mod: '../routes/billing.js' }`), which runs during route registration — after applyCoreMiddleware has already installed `app.use('/api', express.json({ limit: '2mb' }))` at server/startup/middleware.ts:74. Stripe sends `Content-Type: application/json`, so express.json consumes the stream and sets `req._body = true`; body-parser's raw() then short-circuits and leaves req.body as a parsed object. No rawBody capture exists anywhere (grepped `rawBody` and `verify:` across server/startup/middleware.ts, server/middleware/enterprise-security.ts and server/bootstrap/register-inline-routes.ts — zero hits). Contrast the firecrawl webhook, which IS correctly mounted before the parser at server/startup/middleware.ts:70. I reproduced this with the repo's own express 5.2.1 and stripe 18.5.0, replicating the mount order exactly and sending a correctly HMAC-signed payload: `typeof req.body = object, isBuffer = false` and `HTTP 400 {"error":"Invalid signature", detail:"Webhook payload must be provided as a string or a Buffer … Payload was provided as a parsed JavaScript object instead."}`.

**Failure scenario.** Stripe POSTs `customer.subscription.deleted` (or `invoice.payment_failed`, or `customer.subscription.updated` on a downgrade) with a valid `stripe-signature` header. constructEvent throws on the suspect payload type, the catch at billing.ts:282-296 returns 400, and processWebhookEvent is never reached. Stripe retries for ~3 days, gets 400 every time, and disables the endpoint. The direct consequence for a life-sciences SaaS: a customer who cancels or whose card declines retains full platform access to their regulatory dossiers and all paid features indefinitely, because nothing else in the codebase revokes entitlements — server/services/lifecycle/org-lifecycle.ts:24 documents the Stripe webhook as the sole trigger for the `billing_active` transition. It is fail-closed against forgery (an attacker cannot get a forged event accepted either), so this is a revenue-integrity and entitlement-enforcement defect rather than a data breach — but it is silent, and the existing test at server/bootstrap/__tests__/api-auth-gate.test.ts:107 only asserts the path is auth-exempt, not that verification succeeds.

**Fix.** Mount the Stripe webhook before the global JSON parser, exactly as firecrawl is at server/startup/middleware.ts:70 — e.g. `app.use('/api/billing/webhooks/stripe', express.raw({ type: 'application/json' }), stripeWebhookRouter)` inside applyCoreMiddleware. Alternatively add a `verify: (req, _res, buf) => { req.rawBody = buf }` callback to the /api express.json at line 74 and pass `req.rawBody` to constructEvent. Add an integration test that generates a header with `stripe.webhooks.generateTestHeaderString` and asserts the endpoint returns 200 with `{received:true}` — the current test suite would not have caught this.

#### `P2` SC-05 — Firecrawl webhook writes attacker-controlled unbounded data into the compliance audit table BEFORE verifying the signature

`server/routes/firecrawl-webhooks.ts:15` · blocks **G2** · ~hours

**Evidence.** The handler accepts `express.text({ type: '*/*', limit: '2mb' })` (line 9), computes `isValid = verifyFirecrawlWebhook(rawPayload, signature)` at line 12, then unconditionally executes `await pool.query('INSERT INTO external_tool_audit_log (tenant_id, actor_user_id, event_type, event_payload_json, created_at) VALUES ($1,$2,$3,$4,NOW())', [Number((req as any).tenantId || 0), null, isValid ? 'firecrawl_webhook_received' : 'webhook_verification_failed', JSON.stringify({ rawPayload, signaturePresent: Boolean(signature) })])` at lines 15-24. The 401 rejection is at line 26 — AFTER the write. The route is mounted at server/startup/middleware.ts:70, ahead of the auth boundary (server/index.ts:123) and correctly allowlisted at server/middleware/authBoundary.ts:91, so it is reachable unauthenticated from the internet. `req.tenantId` is never populated this early in the chain, so every row lands on tenant_id 0, which is NOT NULL in the schema (sql/migrations/20260327_external_evidence_intelligence.sql:82-89) and belongs to no organization. Compensating controls confirmed present but insufficient: the HMAC comparison itself is correct (constant-time with a length pre-check, server/integrations/firecrawl/client.ts:61-68) and fails closed when FIRECRAWL_WEBHOOK_SECRET is unset (server/integrations/firecrawl/webhook.ts:5); the Redis limiter does run first, but getCategory('/firecrawl-webhooks') matches no segment and falls through to the 'api' bucket at 100 req/min.

**Failure scenario.** An unauthenticated attacker sends `POST /api/firecrawl-webhooks` with any Content-Type (the parser is `type: '*/*'`), no signature header, and a 2 MB body of attacker-chosen text. The server writes a 2 MB JSONB row into external_tool_audit_log and only then returns 401. At the 100 req/min the 'api' bucket permits, that is ~200 MB/minute of unbounded growth in a table that is part of the platform's external-tool compliance evidence, all attributed to a non-existent tenant 0 that no tenant-scoped query or RLS policy will ever surface for review. Because per SC-02 that 100/min bucket is keyed to the shared ALB address rather than the attacker, the same requests simultaneously consume the entire platform's general /api quota, 429-ing legitimate traffic for every tenant. Separately, the signature scheme carries no timestamp or nonce (client.ts:63 signs the payload alone), so any captured valid webhook can be replayed forever; the replay consequence is limited today only because the handler does nothing with the payload beyond logging it, which will stop being true the moment real processing is added.

**Fix.** Move the signature check ahead of the INSERT: verify first, return 401 immediately on failure, and record failures as a bounded counter or a truncated fingerprint (e.g. sha256 of the payload plus its length) rather than the full body. Drop the 2 MB text limit to Firecrawl's realistic maximum. Resolve tenant_id from the verified webhook payload rather than defaulting to 0, and reject rather than write when it cannot be resolved. Add replay protection — require a signed timestamp header within a tolerance window (the same shape Stripe's constructEvent already gives you) and reject duplicate signature values seen inside that window.

#### `P2` SC-06 — 0 of 140 GitHub Actions references are SHA-pinned, and the one job holding production AWS credentials is the only deploy job with no environment approval gate

`.github/workflows/deploy-aws.yml:155` · blocks **G2** · ~days

**Evidence.** Across 21 workflows there are 140 `uses:` references and zero pinned to a 40-character commit SHA — every one is a mutable tag (49x actions/checkout@v4, 30x actions/setup-node@v4, 7x aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials@v4, 4x aws-actions/amazon-ecr-login@v2, 4x aquasecurity/trivy-action@v0.36.0, plus returntocorp/semgrep-action@v1, peter-evans/create-pull-request@v6, bridgecrewio/checkov-action@v12, hashicorp/setup-terraform@v3, 3x neondatabase/*). The realistic attack targets deploy-aws.yml. The `build-push` job begins at line 137 and calls `aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials@v4` at line 155 with `role-to-assume: ${{ secrets.AWS_DEPLOY_ROLE_ARN }}` and `aws-actions/amazon-ecr-login@v2` at line 162, under the workflow-level `permissions: id-token: write` (line 27). Critically, build-push carries NO `environment:` key — the four jobs that do (migrate at line 249, deploy-api at 448, deploy-worker at 540, deploy-frontend at 616) all declare `environment: production`, so the human-approval gate exists but sits entirely downstream of the job that mints AWS credentials. Since build-push successfully reads AWS_DEPLOY_ROLE_ARN without an environment, that secret is repo-scoped and therefore reachable without approval. The repo's own terraform-compliance.yml:19-21 and :33-34 already carry `TODO(GA-blocker): pin every uses: to a full commit SHA. Floating tags are supply-chain-mutable and must be SHA-pinned before GA.' — the risk is acknowledged and unremediated. Two aggravating details: the Trivy security gate itself runs from mutable tags (deploy-aws.yml:118 and :127), so a moved tag turns the blocking CRITICAL/HIGH scan into a silent pass with no other signal; and pr-checks.yml:22 still uses `returntocorp/semgrep-action@v1`, an abandoned action under a GitHub org that Semgrep Inc. has since renamed away from — a dormant repo in a vacated namespace is the single highest-probability takeover target in the set (the repo's own semgrep.yml already moved off it to the semgrep/semgrep container).

**Failure scenario.** A maintainer account at aws-actions is phished, or the `v4` tag is force-moved (which is what mutable major tags permit by design, and requires no action from this repo). The next `git push` of a `v*` release tag runs deploy-aws.yml. In the build-push job the attacker's action executes with ACTIONS_ID_TOKEN_REQUEST_URL and ACTIONS_ID_TOKEN_REQUEST_TOKEN in its environment — it is literally the action whose job is to exchange those for AWS credentials. It mints the OIDC token, calls sts:AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity against AWS_DEPLOY_ROLE_ARN, and pushes a backdoored image to the c2c-prod-api ECR repository. No human approval is required because build-push has no environment gate. The subsequent deploy-api job (which does have one) then deploys that image, and an approver reviewing it sees only a normal-looking digest. Result: code execution inside the production API task holding every customer's regulatory dossiers, plus that task role's s3:GetObject/PutObject/ListBucket on the dossier buckets (terraform/modules/ecs-fargate/main.tf:85-89). No OIDC trust policy is defined in this repo's terraform (grepped token.actions.githubusercontent.com across terraform/ — zero hits), so there is no in-repo evidence that the deploy role's `sub` condition constrains which repository or ref may assume it.

**Fix.** SHA-pin all 140 references (`actions/checkout@<40-hex> # v4.2.2`) and enable Dependabot's github-actions ecosystem in .github/dependabot.yml — it currently covers only npm and pip — so pins get bumped with a reviewable diff. Add `environment: production` to the build-push job so the credential-minting step sits behind the same approval as the deploys, or move AWS_DEPLOY_ROLE_ARN to an environment-scoped secret so a job without the environment cannot read it. Delete the returntocorp/semgrep-action@v1 step in pr-checks.yml:22 — semgrep.yml already provides that coverage via the semgrep/semgrep container (which should itself be digest-pinned rather than `:latest`). Verify out-of-band that the deploy role's trust policy pins `sub` to `repo:concept2cure/Concept2Cure.RI-2-replit:ref:refs/tags/v*` or an environment claim.

#### `P2` SC-07 — Shipped self-host stack publishes an unauthenticated Redis and the dossier Postgres on 0.0.0.0

`docker-compose.yml:44` · blocks **G2** · ~hours

**Evidence.** docker-compose.yml describes itself (lines 3-14) as 'Canonical self-host / local install stack… the install SKU', so this is what a customer deploying on-premises or on their own cloud VM runs. The `redis` service (line 40) uses `image: redis:7-alpine` and publishes `- '${REDIS_PORT:-6379}:6379'` (line 44) with no `requirepass`, no `--requirepass` in a command override, and no password in the app's connection string (`REDIS_URL: redis://redis:6379`, line 74) — grepping requirepass/REDIS_PASSWORD across all four compose files, .env.example and helm/ returns zero hits. The `db` service publishes `- '${DB_PORT:-5432}:5432'` (line 28). Neither is behind a `profiles:` key, so a plain `docker compose up` exposes both. Docker port publishing binds 0.0.0.0 and inserts DOCKER-chain iptables rules that bypass a host's INPUT-chain firewall rules, so 'we have a firewall' is not a mitigation here. Under the `ingestion` profile, opensearch additionally runs with `plugins.security.disabled=true` on port 9200 (lines 111-122). By contrast the AWS-hosted topology is correctly scoped — ECS tasks admit ingress only from the ALB security group (terraform/modules/ecs-fargate/main.tf:99-105) and the ALB admits only 80/443 (terraform/modules/alb/main.tf:85-99) — so this exposure is specific to the customer-run install SKU.

**Failure scenario.** A customer stands up the install SKU on a cloud VM per the shipped compose file. An internet-wide scan finds port 6379 open. The attacker connects with redis-cli, needs no credentials, and reads the platform's cached session and rate-limit state. Redis then gives up remote code execution by the standard route: `CONFIG SET dir /var/lib/redis`, `CONFIG SET dbfilename authorized_keys`, `SET x '<attacker ssh pubkey>'`, `SAVE` — writing an SSH key to the host and yielding a shell on the machine that also runs the app container and the Postgres volume. Independently, port 5432 accepts direct connections to the regulatory-dossier database with whatever DB_PASSWORD the operator set in .env, with no VPC or security-group boundary in front of it and no rate limit on authentication attempts.

**Fix.** Bind both to loopback in the default stack — `'127.0.0.1:${DB_PORT:-5432}:5432'` and `'127.0.0.1:${REDIS_PORT:-6379}:6379'` — since the app reaches them over the `c2c` bridge network by service name and does not need host publishing at all. Add `command: ['redis-server','--requirepass','${REDIS_PASSWORD}']` and carry the password into REDIS_URL, making REDIS_PASSWORD a required .env variable alongside DB_PASSWORD and JWT_SECRET. Do the same for the opensearch/tika/grobid ports under the ingestion profile, and either enable the OpenSearch security plugin or document that the profile is loopback-only.

#### `P2` SC-08 — chromadb CVE-2026-45829: the suppression's unrecorded determination is correct — but the pin is dead code a documented deploy path still installs

`.trivyignore:24` · blocks **G2** · ~hours

**Evidence.** This is the determination the .trivyignore TODO at lines 22-23 asks for ('confirm chromadb is not exposed to untrusted pre-auth network input'), established three independent ways. (1) Nothing imports it: `chromadb` appears exactly once in the entire non-node_modules tree — server/services/python/requirements.txt:37 (`chromadb==1.5.9`) — and grepping `import chromadb|from chromadb|chroma_client|Chroma(` across all 45 .py files in the repo returns zero hits. The directory server/services/python/ contains the requirements.txt and nothing else — no Python source at all. (2) No Python service runs: server/index.ts:192 calls `startPythonBackend()`, and that function at server/startup/services.ts:30-32 is a stub whose entire body is `return Promise.resolve(null);`. (3) It is never installed in the deployed artifact: Dockerfile.optimized — the image deploy-aws.yml builds and pushes to ECR — contains zero `pip install` lines; the only pip invocation in any Dockerfile is services/Dockerfile:13 (`pip install --no-cache-dir python-docx==0.8.11`), and no CI workflow installs server/services/python/requirements.txt (the workflows install the root requirements.txt, services/requirements.txt, or explicit package lists — neither of which contains chromadb). Trivy flags it only because a filesystem scan of `.` discovers every requirements.txt on disk regardless of whether anything installs it. The residual exposure is documentation: docs/deployment/deployment-guide.md:44 lists `pip install --no-cache-dir -r server/services/python/requirements.txt` as step 1 of 'Method 2: Manual Deployment'.

**Failure scenario.** The pre-auth RCE itself is not reachable in the containerized deployment — chromadb is absent from the image and no process would import or serve it. The realistic harm is narrower and worth stating precisely: an operator following the repo's own documented manual-deploy procedure runs the pip command at docs/deployment/deployment-guide.md:44 and installs chromadb 1.5.9 with a known unpatched pre-auth RCE — plus torch 2.12, transformers 5.9, sentence-transformers, celery and redis clients, roughly a gigabyte of packages the application never imports — onto the host that also runs the production Node process. No listener is started, so there is no direct path from the internet; but a known-vulnerable package now sits on the production host's import path, and any future code or operator action that runs `chroma run` exposes it immediately. The compounding cost is that this dead pin permanently occupies a CVE suppression, and every future security review must re-derive the reachability analysis from scratch because the conclusion was never written down.

**Fix.** Delete server/services/python/requirements.txt outright — it pins 40 packages for a service that does not exist and whose launcher is a `return Promise.resolve(null)` stub. That removes CVE-2026-45829 from the scan surface entirely, which is strictly better than suppressing it, and lets you delete the .trivyignore entry at lines 17-24 rather than renewing it. Simultaneously fix docs/deployment/deployment-guide.md:44 to reference requirements.txt (the root file, which the ingestion/OCR path actually uses) or drop the step. If the file is retained for future work, move it out of the Trivy scan path and record the reachability determination above in the .trivyignore comment so the next reviewer does not repeat this analysis.

#### `P2` SC-09 — AI provider spend is metered but never capped — recordCost writes to a Map nothing ever reads

`server/services/ai-gateway/policy.ts:366` · blocks **G2** · ~days

**Evidence.** The policy engine declares `private dailyCost: Map<string, { date: string; total: number }> = new Map();` at line 222 and `recordCost(orgId, cost)` at lines 366-377 accumulates per-org daily spend into it. Grepping `dailyCost` across the entire server tree returns exactly three hits — the declaration at :222 and two writes inside recordCost at :371 and :375. It is never read, and no daily budget check appears in `evaluate()` (lines 232-252), which runs only checkTokenBudget, checkPromptInjection, checkBlockedPatterns and checkRateLimit. There is no spend ceiling anywhere: grepping DAILY_BUDGET|MAX_DAILY|spendCap|maxSpend|AI_BUDGET|budgetUsd|costLimit across server/ and .env.example returns zero hits. The only controls on the dispatch path are checkTokenBudget (a per-request maxTokens ceiling of 128,000, line 95) and checkRateLimit (lines 515-556), whose buckets live in an in-process Map — so with api_desired_count 2 the stated 100 req/min/org and 30 req/min/user limits are actually 200 and 60 in aggregate, and both reset to zero on every deploy or task restart. Partial compensating control confirmed: checkQuota/recordUsage from server/services/usage-metering.ts IS enforced on specific expensive features (server/services/deep-research-orchestrator.ts:13, server/services/csr-builder.ts:13, server/routes/public-api.ts:24), but it is not applied at the gateway chokepoint, so any AI surface that does not opt in has no quota at all. The credit ledger (server/services/credit-ledger.ts) is never consulted on any dispatch path — grepping consumeCredits|deductCredits|checkCredits|reserveCredits across server/ returns zero hits.

**Failure scenario.** A single authenticated user of one tenant scripts the AnA/document-generation surface at 30 requests per minute per process (60 across the two tasks), each with maxTokens up to 128,000 against a frontier model. Nothing denies the request on cost grounds at any point: dailyCost silently accumulates in a Map that no code path inspects, no alert fires, and the first signal is the monthly Anthropic/OpenAI invoice. Because the buckets are per-process and in-memory, a routine deploy resets the counters mid-attack. For a platform whose cost of goods is dominated by LLM inference, one compromised or malicious tenant account can convert an unbounded amount of the vendor's gross margin into provider spend, and the platform's own instrumentation is structurally incapable of noticing.

**Fix.** Read the meter you already write: add a `checkDailyBudget` step to evaluate() at policy.ts:232 that denies when dailyCost for the org exceeds a configured per-org ceiling, and surface the ceiling in PolicyConfig alongside maxTokensPerRequest. Move dailyCost and the rateBuckets out of process memory into Redis (the platform already has a Redis client and a sliding-window implementation in server/middleware/redisRateLimiter.ts) so limits are correct across tasks and survive restarts. Export the accumulated per-org cost as a Prometheus gauge through the existing /api/metrics surface and alert on the rate of change — the scrape endpoint and auth for it already exist.

#### `P3` SC-10 — /readyz discloses database schema-provisioning state to unauthenticated callers

`server/startup/inline-endpoints.ts:114` · ~hours

**Evidence.** mountFastPathHealthEndpoints is invoked at server/index.ts:110, before applyCoreMiddleware and well before the auth boundary, and /readyz is a non-/api path so the boundary at server/index.ts:123 never covers it — server/middleware/authBoundary.ts:30 acknowledges this explicitly ('/healthz, /readyz — fast-path liveness probes (no tenant data)'). On failure the handler returns `{ ready: false, failed, dependencies: deps }` and, when the schema check fails, adds `body.schemaDetail = getSchemaReadinessDetail()` (line 114). That detail string is composed in server/startup/services.ts:62 and :75 as `missing tables: ${result.missingCritical.join(', ')}` or `${failingSubsystem.name} subsystem ${failingSubsystem.state} (missing: ${failingSubsystem.missing.join(', ')})`. The boundary's 'no tenant data' claim is accurate — no customer records are exposed — but internal table and subsystem names are. A related, milder case: /api/ai-gateway/health (inline-endpoints.ts:535-559) is on the public allowlist (authBoundary.ts:79) and returns getProviderHealth(), which per server/services/ai-gateway/types.ts:460-469 carries provider name, healthy flag, consecutiveFailures, avgLatencyMs, requestCount and errorRate — no credentials, but it does tell an unauthenticated caller which LLM providers are configured and whether they are currently degraded. Correctly handled by contrast: /api/shadow/health is deliberately NOT allowlisted and is therefore covered by the default-deny boundary.

**Failure scenario.** An attacker probing a newly provisioned or partially migrated environment GETs /readyz and receives, for example, `{"ready":false,"failed":["schema"],"schemaDetail":"authoring subsystem PARTIAL (missing: ectd_sections, submission_sequences, esignature_records)"}`. That names internal tables and identifies which regulated subsystems are incompletely provisioned — a precise map of where to aim, and a reliable signal that the environment is mid-migration and least likely to be monitored. Low severity because it requires the environment to already be in a degraded state and leaks no customer data, but it is free reconnaissance on an endpoint that only needs to answer yes or no.

**Fix.** Keep the boolean and the dependency roll-up in the public response and move schemaDetail behind requireMetricsAuth — the guard already exists at server/startup/inline-endpoints.ts:39-55 and is what /api/metrics and /api/health/full use. Alternatively log the detail server-side and return an opaque correlation id, matching the pattern already applied correctly at inline-endpoints.ts:135-141 for the health-check exception path.
